# Paper III English (Q1-100) and Odia (Q101-200), OPSC Assistant Section Officer (Office of the Advocate General, Odisha) written exam of 9 November 2025, Advt. No. 02 of 2025-26, Series A (OPSC's own booklet); answers: OPSC's final answer key of 17 January 2026

Q 1 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The intricate design ______ meticulous attention during manufacturing.
(1) calls for
(2) comes to
(3) gets over
(4) puts off
= 1
~ "Calls for" is the right phrasal verb: "call for" means to require, need or demand. An intricate (very detailed, complicated) design naturally requires meticulous (very careful, precise) attention, so "The intricate design calls for meticulous attention during manufacturing" makes perfect sense. "Comes to" means to reach a total or to regain consciousness ("The bill comes to ₹500"), which does not fit. "Gets over" means to recover from something ("She got over her illness"), and "puts off" means to postpone or to discourage ("They put off the meeting"); neither can take "attention" in this sense. Another example of the rule: "This situation calls for quick action." Tip: a phrasal verb can change its meaning completely with the particle, so learn them in sets: call for (require), call off (cancel), call on (visit), call up (phone or summon). Trap: "come to" looks similar in form but refers to totals, decisions or waking up.

Q 2 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
Each of the buildings ______ unique design.
(1) have
(2) has
(3) are
(4) were
= 2
~ "Has" is correct because "each" is singular and takes a singular verb. In "Each of the buildings has a unique design", the real subject is "Each" (each one), and "of the buildings" is only a phrase describing it, so the verb agrees with "Each", not with "buildings". "Have" is the plural form, "are" is plural and also gives no sense of possession, and "were" is plural past tense. The same rule applies to every, either, neither, one of, everyone and each one: "One of my friends lives in Cuttack"; "Neither of the answers is correct." The printed sentence leaves out the article "a" before "unique design", but that does not change the choice of verb: only "has" agrees with the subject. Trap: don't let the plural noun just before the verb ("buildings") pull the verb into the plural; this "proximity" error is a favourite trap in competitive exams.

Q 3 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The mineral deposits lie ______, beyond the reach of conventional mining.
(1) subterranean
(2) subterraneously
(3) extraterrestrial
(4) insubordinately
= 2
~ The key's answer is "subterraneously", meaning beneath the surface of the earth, underground. The idea behind it is that the blank tells us where or how the deposits lie, so an adverb is expected after the verb "lie", just as in "lie underground" or "lie deep". It also matches the clue "beyond the reach of conventional mining": ordinary mining cannot reach deposits buried that far down. "Extraterrestrial" means from outside the Earth (like meteorites), and "insubordinately" means disobediently, so neither makes sense here. The word comes from Latin sub (under) + terra (earth), the same root as terrain and territory; the adjective form is "subterranean" ("subterranean rivers", "a subterranean tunnel"). Note: "subterraneously" is a rare word, and the adjective "subterranean" can also be defended in this sentence, because "lie" can be followed by an adjective describing a state, as in "lie dormant", "lie hidden" or "lie idle". (Official key; some sources differ.)

Q 4 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
It was ______ honour to meet the author.
(1) a
(2) an
(3) the
(4) some
= 2
~ "An" is correct because the choice between "a" and "an" depends on the first sound of the next word, not its first letter. In "honour" the h is silent, so the word begins with a vowel sound ("on-er"), and vowel sounds take "an": "It was an honour to meet the author." "A" is used before consonant sounds, so "a honour" is wrong. "The" would point to a particular, already-known honour, which does not suit the general sense of "It was an honour to ...", and "some" is used with uncountable or plural nouns and would change the meaning to "a certain amount of honour". Other silent-h words that take "an": an hour, an honest man, an heir, an honorarium. The reverse case: words that start with a vowel letter but a consonant sound take "a": a university, a European, a one-day match, a unique idea. Trap: "a hotel", "a historic event" and "a hospital" take "a" because their h is pronounced.

Q 5 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The concert was so ______ that many people were moved to tears.
(1) chaotic
(2) touching
(3) dull
(4) loud
= 2
~ "Touching" fits because it means emotionally moving, stirring tender feelings such as sympathy or joy. The sentence uses the "so ... that" pattern, in which the second part shows the result of the first: the concert was so touching that people were moved to tears. "Chaotic" (disorderly, confused) might upset people but would not move them to tears; "dull" (boring) would leave them unmoved; and "loud" describes volume, not feeling. Synonyms of touching worth knowing: moving, poignant, heart-warming, stirring. Example: "The farewell speech of the retiring teacher was so touching that the whole class wept." Vocabulary tip: the adjective "touching" (emotionally moving) is different from the verb "touch" (make contact), and "touchy" means easily offended, a completely different word. Trap: in such sentences always look for the clue phrase, here "moved to tears", and pick the word whose meaning produces that result rather than one that merely describes a concert.

Q 6 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The novel is known for its ______ style, which contrasts sharply with the author's earlier complex work.
(1) simple
(2) happy
(3) intricate
(4) shallow
= 1
~ "Simple" is correct because the sentence sets up a contrast: the novel's style "contrasts sharply with the author's earlier complex work", so the blank needs the opposite of "complex", which is "simple" (plain, easy to follow). "Intricate" means complicated or full of detail, the same as complex, so there would be no contrast at all. "Happy" describes mood or tone, not how complex a style is. "Shallow" means lacking depth and is a negative judgement, whereas the sentence presents the style as something the novel "is known for"; the natural opposite of a complex style is a simple or lucid one, not a shallow one. Contrast signals to watch for: unlike, whereas, while, but, although, in contrast to, contrasts with. Example: "Unlike his long speeches, his letters were short and simple." Antonym pairs useful for exams: complex/simple, intricate/plain, verbose/concise, obscure/lucid. Trap: picking "intricate" because it sounds literary; read the contrast clue first.

Q 7 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
She ______ her initial enthusiasm, giving her team a positive outlook.
(1) gave up
(2) held on to
(3) put up with
(4) got over
= 2
~ "Held on to" is correct: to hold on to something means to keep it and not let it go. She kept her early enthusiasm, and that is why her team got a positive outlook: "She held on to her initial enthusiasm, giving her team a positive outlook." "Gave up" means abandoned or stopped ("she gave up smoking"), which would leave the team discouraged, not positive. "Got over" means recovered from something unpleasant ("got over the shock"), and one does not need to recover from enthusiasm. "Put up with" means tolerated or endured ("put up with the noise"), used for something annoying, not for a good feeling. Another example: "Despite the losses, the captain held on to his belief in the young players." Tip: the participle phrase "giving her team a positive outlook" shows the result, so choose the verb that produces a positive result. Trap: "got over" and "gave up" both suggest losing the feeling, the opposite of what the sentence needs.

Q 8 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
Her final exam results were ______ surprising, even to her parents.
(1) meticulously
(2) fast
(3) slowly
(4) hardly
= 4
~ "Hardly" is the right word: it is an adverb of degree meaning "scarcely" or "almost not", and it can modify an adjective such as "surprising". "Her final exam results were hardly surprising" means the results were expected, since she had been doing well. "Meticulously" (very carefully) and "slowly" are adverbs of manner that describe how an action is done, so results cannot be "meticulously surprising" or "slowly surprising"; "fast" describes speed and is not used before an adjective like this. Remember that "hardly" already carries a negative sense, so don't add another negative: say "I can hardly believe it", not "I can't hardly believe it". Also don't confuse "hardly" (scarcely) with "hard" (with effort): "He worked hard" and "He hardly worked" have opposite meanings. Similar degree adverbs: barely, scarcely, rather, quite. Trap: "-ly" alone does not make a word fit; check whether the adverb can sensibly describe the adjective that follows.

Q 9 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The pair of shoes ______ on sale at the store.
(1) is
(2) are
(3) be
(4) were
= 1
~ "Is" is correct because the true subject is "pair", which is singular; "of shoes" only tells us what the pair consists of. So the verb must be singular and, since the sale is happening now, in the present tense: "The pair of shoes is on sale at the store." "Are" and "were" are plural forms, and "were" is also past tense; "be" is the base form and cannot follow a singular subject in a normal statement. The same rule works for other "a pair of" and "a set of" phrases: "A pair of scissors is on the table", "A set of books was gifted to the library", "A bouquet of flowers looks lovely." But nouns such as shoes, trousers, scissors and spectacles take a plural verb when used alone: "My shoes are new." Trap: the plural noun "shoes" sits right before the verb and tempts you to write "are"; always find the head noun of the subject first.

Q 10 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
They ______ the Asia Cup trophy in 1993.
(1) win
(2) has won
(3) won
(4) winning
= 3
~ "Won" is correct: when a sentence names a definite past time, such as "in 1993", "yesterday", "last year" or "two days ago", the simple past tense is used: "They won the Asia Cup trophy in 1993." The present perfect "has won" links the past to the present and cannot be used with a specific past date; it is also wrong in number, because "They" needs "have", not "has". "Win" is present tense and clashes with 1993, and "winning" is a participle that needs a helping verb ("were winning"). Compare: "India has won the Asia Cup several times" (no time stated, so the present perfect is fine) with "India won the Asia Cup in 2023" (definite time, so simple past). Trap: never pair "has/have + past participle" with a finished time expression; "I have seen him yesterday" is a classic error-spotting mistake. Tip: words like already, ever, never, just and since usually go with the present perfect.

Q 11 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The researchers conducted an experiment ______ the effects of climate change.
(1) in
(2) on
(3) under
(4) for
= 2
~ "On" is correct: the noun "experiment" takes "on" (or "into") to introduce the subject being studied: "The researchers conducted an experiment on the effects of climate change." The same preposition follows similar nouns of study: research on (or into), a study on, a report on, a lecture on, a book on, a thesis on. "In" shows a field or place ("an experiment in chemistry", "in the laboratory"); "under" shows conditions or control ("under supervision", "under pressure"); and "for" shows purpose or benefit ("an experiment for students"). None of these introduces the topic of the experiment. Note: we also say "experiment with" when trying out something new ("experiment with new teaching methods"), and "experiment on" when testing something, as in "experiments on animals". Trap: don't pick "in" just because "effects of climate change" sounds like a field of study; ask what the experiment is about, and that needs "on".

Q 12 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
In retrospect, I wish I ______ more time for this project.
(1) have
(2) had
(3) will have
(4) having
= 2
~ "Had" is the answer. After "I wish", an unreal wish or regret is shown by moving the verb one tense back: a wish about the present uses the past simple ("I wish I had more time", meaning I don't have enough time now), and a wish about the past uses the past perfect ("I wish I had had more time", meaning I didn't have enough then). "In retrospect" (looking back) points to a past regret, so "had had" would be the most precise form, but it is not among the options; of the four, only "had" follows the wish pattern. "Have" is plain present tense, which "wish" does not take for unreal situations; "will have" is future and cannot follow "wish" in this way; and "having" is a participle, not a finite verb. More examples: "She wishes she knew the answer"; "I wish I were taller"; "He wishes he had studied harder." Trap: never use "will" after "wish"; use "would" for wishes about other people's actions ("I wish you would stop").

Q 13 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
His explanation was so ______ that everyone understood the difficult concept.
(1) complicated
(2) clear
(3) confusing
(4) vague
= 2
~ "Clear" is correct: a clear explanation is easy to understand, so the result "everyone understood the difficult concept" follows naturally. The sentence uses the "so + adjective + that + result" pattern, where the adjective must logically cause the result. "Complicated" and "confusing" would make the concept harder to grasp, and "vague" means unclear or imprecise, the opposite of what the result shows. Synonyms of clear worth knowing: lucid, explicit, intelligible, transparent; antonyms: vague, ambiguous, obscure, convoluted. Another example of the pattern: "The road was so slippery that several bikes skidded." Exam tip: in sentence-completion questions, read the whole sentence first, find the clue (here "everyone understood"), and predict a word before looking at the options. Trap: the word "difficult" in the sentence might pull you towards "complicated", but it describes the concept, not the explanation; the explanation must be the thing that made the difficult concept easy.

Q 14 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
His ______ nature always brings laughter to the group.
(1) indifferent
(2) humorous
(3) gloomy
(4) serious
= 2
~ "Humorous" is correct: it means funny or full of humour, and a funny nature naturally "brings laughter to the group". The blank needs an adjective describing his nature that causes laughter. "Indifferent" means uninterested or unconcerned, which brings no laughter; "gloomy" means sad or depressing; and "serious" means solemn and grave. All three would dampen a group rather than amuse it. Spelling note: "humour" (the British spelling used in Indian exams) drops the u in the adjective: humorous, not "humourous". Similar words: witty (clever and quick with humour), jovial (cheerful and friendly), amusing, comical. Don't confuse "humorous" (funny) with "humane" (kind) or "humid" (moist). Example: "Her humorous remarks eased the tension in the meeting." Tip: let the clue in the sentence decide; "brings laughter" points to a light-hearted, positive quality, so the three negative or neutral words can be struck out at once.

Q 15 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
Although Mala is the oldest child in her group, she is the ______ of all.
(1) innocent
(2) more innocent
(3) most innocent
(4) innocently
= 3
~ "Most innocent" is correct because the phrase "the ... of all" compares Mala with everyone in her group, that is, with more than two people, which calls for the superlative degree: "Although Mala is the oldest child in her group, she is the most innocent of all." The sentence itself shows the pattern, since "the oldest" is also a superlative. "Innocent" is the positive degree and cannot fill "the ... of all"; "more innocent" is the comparative, used when comparing two and usually followed by "than" ("She is more innocent than her sister"); and "innocently" is an adverb, which cannot describe a person after "is". Rule of degrees: short adjectives add -er/-est (old, older, oldest), while longer ones such as innocent or beautiful use more/most (more beautiful, most beautiful). Note the contrast signalled by "Although": oldest in age, yet the most innocent in nature. Trap: avoid double forms like "more older" or "most oldest".

Q 16 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
His ______ outlook on life is refreshing, unlike the pessimism of his brothers.
(1) dark
(2) positive
(3) negative
(4) grim
= 2
~ "Positive" is correct. The phrase "unlike the pessimism of his brothers" signals a contrast, so his outlook must be the opposite of pessimistic, that is, hopeful or optimistic, and "positive" expresses exactly that. It also fits the word "refreshing", which shows approval. "Dark", "negative" and "grim" all mean gloomy or hopeless, which is the same as pessimism, so they would destroy the contrast created by "unlike". Word pair to remember: optimism (expecting good outcomes) versus pessimism (expecting the worst); a person is an optimist or a pessimist. Other contrast markers: whereas, while, but, in contrast to, on the other hand. Example: "Unlike her anxious classmates, she remained calm before the exam." Trap: in contrast sentences, three options often share one meaning (here dark, negative and grim); the odd one out is usually the answer. Tip: "outlook" means a general attitude to life, so look for a word that describes attitude.

Q 17 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
This will prevent companies ______ hazardous waste improperly.
(1) disposed
(2) to dispose
(3) from disposing
(4) dispose
= 3
~ "From disposing" is correct because the verb "prevent" follows the fixed pattern "prevent + object + from + -ing": "This will prevent companies from disposing of hazardous waste improperly." Verbs of stopping or keeping away take this pattern: prevent, stop, prohibit, deter, discourage, restrain, keep: "The rain prevented us from playing"; "The law prohibits shops from selling tobacco to minors." "To dispose" is wrong because "prevent" is not followed by a to-infinitive, and "disposed" (past form) and "dispose" (base form) break the pattern altogether. Note also that "dispose" normally takes "of" when it means getting rid of something: dispose of waste. Strictly, the sentence reads best as "from disposing of hazardous waste", but among the options C is the only correct structure. Trap: don't mix up "avoid" and "prevent": you avoid doing something yourself ("avoid wasting water"), but you prevent someone else from doing it.

Q 18 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
We meet ______, in June and in December to review the progress.
(1) annually
(2) biannually
(3) weekly
(4) monthly
= 2
~ "Biannually" is correct: it means twice a year, and meeting "in June and in December" is exactly twice a year, about six months apart. "Annually" means once a year, while "weekly" and "monthly" mean every week and every month, far more often than the two named months. Be careful with the confusing pair: biannual or biannually = twice a year (also called half-yearly or semi-annually), while biennial or biennially = once every two years. Some dictionaries note that "biannual" is occasionally used loosely for "every two years", which is why official writing often prefers the clearer "half-yearly". Other frequency words: fortnightly (every two weeks), quarterly (every three months), triennial (every three years), centenary (a hundredth anniversary). Example: "The board publishes its accounts biannually, in March and September." Trap: "bi-" can mean "two" in both words, so tie biannual to "two times a year" and biennial to "two years".

Q 19 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
______ did the child touch the tree than it broke out into blossoms.
(1) No sooner
(2) Only
(3) Hardly
(4) Narrowly
= 1
~ "No sooner" is correct because the sentence uses "than" in its second half, and the fixed correlative pair is "No sooner ... than". It shows that one action followed immediately after another: "No sooner did the child touch the tree than it broke out into blossoms." When a negative expression like "No sooner" starts the sentence, the auxiliary comes before the subject (inversion): "did the child touch", not "the child touched". "Hardly" and "scarcely" pair with "when", not "than": "Hardly had I reached the station when the train left." "Only" must be followed by a word or phrase such as "then" or "after the exam" and does not pair with "than" ("Only then did I realise ..."), and "Narrowly" means "only just" and makes no sense here. Pairs to memorise: no sooner ... than; hardly/scarcely/barely ... when; not only ... but also; both ... and; either ... or; neither ... nor. Trap: "Hardly ... than" is a common error to spot in error-detection questions.

Q 20 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The team, as well as their coach, ______ optimistic about the season ahead.
(1) are
(2) is
(3) were
(4) be
= 2
~ "Is" is correct. When two subjects are joined by "as well as", "along with", "together with" or "in addition to", the verb agrees with the first subject; the phrase set off by commas is only extra information. Here the first subject is "The team", a collective noun treated as one unit, so the singular verb "is" fits: "The team, as well as their coach, is optimistic about the season ahead." "Are" and "were" are plural forms, so they do not agree with the singular subject "The team"; "be" is the base form and cannot act as the main verb of this statement. Compare: "The students, as well as the teacher, are coming" (first subject plural, so plural verb). Collective nouns such as team, committee, jury and family usually take a singular verb when the group acts as one. Trap: don't count "team" and "coach" together as a plural subject; that happens only when they are joined by "and".

Q 21 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The train ______ before she started from home.
(1) left
(2) has left
(3) had left
(4) will leave
= 3
~ "Had left" is correct. When two actions both happened in the past, the earlier one is put in the past perfect (had + past participle) and the later one in the simple past. Here the train left first and she started from home afterwards, so: "The train had left before she started from home." The simple past "left" is often heard in everyday speech when "before" makes the order clear, but exam grammar expects the past perfect for the earlier action, so "had left" is the best choice. "Has left" (present perfect) cannot be combined with a past-time clause like "before she started", and "will leave" is future, which clashes with "started". More examples: "The patient had died before the doctor arrived"; "When I reached the hall, the lecture had already begun." Memory tip: "had" goes with the action that happened first. Trap: don't put both verbs in the past perfect; only the earlier action takes "had".

Q 22 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
______ does one encounter such six-hitting feat by an Indian batsman.
(1) Infrequent
(2) Hard
(3) Few
(4) Hardly
= 4
~ "Hardly" is correct. It is a negative adverb meaning "almost never" or "scarcely", and when such an adverb begins a sentence, the auxiliary comes before the subject (inversion): "Hardly does one encounter such a six-hitting feat by an Indian batsman", i.e. one hardly ever sees such a feat. The question already shows this inversion, "does one encounter", which only a negative adverb can trigger. "Infrequent" is an adjective, so it cannot open the sentence and cause inversion (the adverb "infrequently" would be needed); "hard" as an adverb means "with effort" ("work hard"), not "almost never"; and "few" is a determiner that needs a plural noun ("Few people ..."). Other inversion starters: never, seldom, rarely, scarcely, little, not only, under no circumstances: "Seldom do we see such rain in April." Trap: "hard" and "hardly" look alike but have unrelated meanings; "He hardly works" means he does very little work.

Q 23 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
Very few students in the class have performed ______ well.
(1) vary
(2) reasonably
(3) perfect
(4) timely
= 2
~ "Reasonably" is correct: it is an adverb of degree meaning "fairly" or "to a satisfactory extent", and an adverb is needed here to modify another adverb, "well". "Very few students in the class have performed reasonably well" means only a small number did fairly well. "Vary" is a verb meaning to change or differ; it is a spelling trap for "very". "Perfect" is an adjective, so it cannot modify "well" (the adverb "perfectly" would be needed). "Timely" looks like an adverb because of its -ly ending but is actually an adjective ("a timely warning"); other such -ly adjectives are friendly, lovely, costly and lonely. Rule: adjectives describe nouns, while adverbs describe verbs, adjectives and other adverbs. Example: "She sings remarkably well." Trap: don't assume every word ending in -ly is an adverb, and read options letter by letter, because "vary" and "very" differ by only one letter.

Q 24 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
Had she known about the meeting, she ______ there on time.
(1) would be
(2) would have been
(3) will be
(4) is
= 2
~ "Would have been" is correct. "Had she known" is the inverted, more formal form of "If she had known", the if-clause of a third conditional, which talks about an unreal past situation (she did not know, so she was not there on time). The main clause of a third conditional takes "would have + past participle": "Had she known about the meeting, she would have been there on time." "Would be" belongs to the second conditional ("If she knew, she would be ..."), about unreal present or future situations; "will be" belongs to the first conditional ("If she knows, she will be ..."), about real possibilities; and "is" is simple present, which cannot follow a past-perfect condition. Conditionals at a glance: Type 1, if + present → will; Type 2, if + past → would; Type 3, if + had + past participle → would have + past participle. Another inverted example: "Had I left earlier, I would have caught the bus." Trap: never put "would" in the if-clause.

Q 25 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
His expertise lies ______ the development of renewable energy technologies.
(1) on
(2) in
(3) for
(4) to
= 2
~ "In" is correct: "lie in" is a fixed expression meaning "to be found in" or "to consist of", used to say where a strength, skill, problem or solution is located: "His expertise lies in the development of renewable energy technologies." Compare: "The problem lies in poor planning"; "Her strength lies in her patience." "On" would suggest a surface or a topic ("a talk on energy"), "for" shows purpose, and "to" shows direction; none of these goes with "lie" in this abstract sense. The noun "expertise" itself also takes "in": "expertise in finance". Related phrases: "lie with" means "be the responsibility of" ("The decision lies with the Commission"), and "lie behind" means "be the hidden cause of". Also remember the verb forms: lie (rest, be situated) – lay – lain, while lay (put down) – laid – laid. Trap: don't confuse "lies in" (consists in) with "lies on", which is mostly used for physical position ("The file lies on the table").

Q 26 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The ______ findings of the research were welcomed by the academic community.
(1) mundane
(2) insightful
(3) superficial
(4) abstract
= 2
~ "Insightful" is correct: it means showing deep and accurate understanding, which is exactly what scholars value, so such findings would be "welcomed by the academic community". The clue is the positive reaction ("were welcomed"), which needs a positive adjective. "Mundane" means ordinary or dull, so mundane findings would hardly be welcomed; "superficial" means shallow or lacking depth, which is a criticism; and "abstract" means theoretical or not concrete, which is neutral and does not explain why the findings were praised. Word family: insight (noun), insightful (adjective), insightfully (adverb). Synonyms: perceptive, penetrating, illuminating; antonyms: superficial, shallow. Example: "The committee's insightful report changed the course of the policy debate." Tip: in a sentence-completion question, first decide whether the blank needs a positive or a negative word; here the welcome tells you it is positive. Trap: "abstract" sounds academic but is not praise in itself.

Q 27 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
His actions were seen as ______, while most expected him to be more considerate.
(1) compassionate
(2) polite
(3) selfish
(4) generous
= 3
~ "Selfish" is correct: it means caring mainly about oneself and not about others. The clause "while most expected him to be more considerate" shows a contrast between what people expected (consideration, thoughtfulness for others) and how his actions appeared, so the blank needs the opposite of considerate, which is "selfish". "Compassionate" (feeling sympathy for others), "polite" (well-mannered) and "generous" (willing to give) are all qualities of a considerate person, so they would remove the contrast. Here "while" means "whereas", a contrast marker, not "during the time that". Antonym pairs useful for exams: selfish/altruistic, considerate/inconsiderate, generous/stingy (or miserly), polite/rude. Example: "Unlike his brother, who is generous, he is quite stingy." Trap: the words "more considerate" tell you his actions were less considerate than expected, so the answer must be a negative word, and only one option is negative.

Q 28 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The house looked ______ as it had an unwelcoming and unattractive air about it.
(1) bleak
(2) pleasant
(3) engaging
(4) enticing
= 1
~ Bleak describes a place or situation that is cold, bare, dreary and uninviting, so it fits the clue in the second half of the sentence, 'an unwelcoming and unattractive air'. In fill-in-the-blank vocabulary questions, the words after 'as', 'because' or 'since' usually explain the blank, so read that part first and look for a word with the same tone. Here the clue is clearly negative. 'Pleasant' means agreeable and nice, the exact opposite of unwelcoming. 'Engaging' means charming and holding your interest, which clashes with 'unattractive'. 'Enticing' means tempting or alluring, again a positive word. Only 'bleak' carries the gloomy, unfriendly sense the sentence demands. Bleak is also used for the future: 'The company's prospects look bleak after the losses' means there is little hope. Related words worth learning: dreary, desolate, grim, forlorn; antonyms: inviting, cheerful, cosy. Tip: when three options share one tone (here positive) and only one has the tone of the clue, that odd one out is usually the answer.

Q 29 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The committee finally ______ at a decision after hours of debate.
(1) arrived
(2) came
(3) went
(4) drew
= 1
~ 'Arrive at' is the fixed phrase meaning to reach something after thought or discussion, so 'The committee finally arrived at a decision after hours of debate' is correct. Verbs that regularly go with particular nouns and prepositions are called collocations, and examiners test them often. We say arrive at a decision, a conclusion, an agreement, a solution or a verdict. 'Came' is wrong because 'come' takes 'to' in this sense: 'came to a decision' is fine, but 'came at a decision' is not English. 'Went' does not collocate with decision at all; 'go at' means to attack or tackle something energetically. 'Drew' pairs with conclusion ('drew a conclusion') but never with 'at a decision'. Also learn the place rule for arrive: arrive at a small place or point (arrive at the station, arrive at the airport) and arrive in a city or country (arrive in Bhubaneswar, arrive in India); never 'arrive to'. Similar collocations: reach a decision, take a decision, make a decision.

Q 30 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The scientists scrutinised the data ______ to ensure accuracy.
(1) thorough
(2) through
(3) meticulously
(4) hardly
= 3
~ Meticulously means with great care and attention to every small detail, and as an adverb it tells us how the scientists scrutinised the data, which suits the purpose 'to ensure accuracy'. Rule: a word that describes an action verb must be an adverb (usually ending in -ly), not an adjective. Here the verb 'scrutinised' already has its object 'the data', so the blank after it needs an adverb of manner. 'Thorough' is an adjective; the adverb would be 'thoroughly', so 'scrutinised the data thorough' is ungrammatical. 'Through' is a preposition (walk through the park) and is often confused with 'thorough' because of the spelling; it makes no sense here. 'Hardly' is an adverb, but it means 'almost not', so 'scrutinised the data hardly to ensure accuracy' contradicts itself. Don't confuse hardly with hard: 'He worked hard' means with great effort, while 'He hardly worked' means he did almost no work. Another example: 'The clerk checked the accounts meticulously before the audit.' Synonyms: carefully, painstakingly, scrupulously.

Q 31 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
A number of factors ______ influenced the outcome of the project.
(1) was
(2) have
(3) has
(4) is
= 2
~ 'A number of factors have influenced the outcome' is correct because 'a number of' means 'many' or 'several', and the real subject is the plural noun 'factors', so the verb must be plural: have. The tense is present perfect (have + past participle), which links past causes to a present result. Compare the trap expression 'the number of', which means the count itself and takes a singular verb: 'The number of factors is large.' Memory tip: A number → are/have (plural); The number → is/has (singular). 'Has' is singular, so it disagrees with 'a number of factors'. 'Was influenced' and 'is influenced' would be passive voice, meaning the factors themselves received the action; but the sentence has an object, 'the outcome', after the verb, and a passive verb cannot take such an object, so both are wrong (they are also singular). Similar examples: 'A number of students were absent today' and 'A lot of people have applied', but 'The number of applicants has doubled.'

Q 32 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
______ did she articulate her views that the audience sat in stunned silence.
(1) So compellingly
(2) Such compelling
(3) How compellingly
(4) Thus compelling
= 1
~ 'So compellingly' is right because the sentence follows the result pattern 'so + adverb + that', and when 'so + adverb' is moved to the front for emphasis, the subject and auxiliary are inverted. Normal order: 'She articulated her views so compellingly that the audience sat in stunned silence.' Emphatic order: 'So compellingly did she articulate her views that ...' Notice 'did she articulate', the question-like word order. Compellingly is an adverb meaning in a powerful, convincing way, and it modifies the verb 'articulate'. 'Such compelling' is wrong because 'such' goes with a noun or adjective + noun ('such compelling arguments that ...'), and an adjective cannot describe the verb. 'How compellingly' is used in exclamations or questions ('How compellingly she spoke!') and does not pair with 'that' to show a result. 'Thus compelling' (therefore convincing) gives neither the adverb nor the so ... that link. Another example: 'So loudly did he shout that the neighbours woke up.' Other inversion starters: Such was ..., Only then ..., Seldom ...

Q 33 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The solution ______ remains controversial, despite earnest efforts.
(1) still
(2) yet
(3) may
(4) not with standing
= 1
~ 'Still' is the adverb that shows a situation continuing up to now, unchanged, so 'The solution still remains controversial, despite earnest efforts' fits: in spite of serious efforts, the controversy has not ended. 'Still' normally goes before the main verb (still remains, still works) and after 'be' (is still pending). 'Yet' also means 'up to now', but in that sense it is used mainly in negative sentences and questions and usually comes at the end: 'The issue has not been solved yet.' In a positive sentence it is unnatural before 'remains'. 'May' is a modal verb and must be followed by the base form, so 'may remains' is ungrammatical (it would need 'may remain'). 'Notwithstanding' (one word, not 'not with standing') is a preposition meaning 'in spite of' and needs a noun phrase with it ('notwithstanding the efforts' or 'the efforts notwithstanding'); placed before a verb it makes no sense, and it would also repeat the idea of 'despite'. Strictly, 'remains' already implies continuity, but 'still remains' is common and accepted in exam English. Another example: 'He is still waiting for the result.'

Q 34 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
She ______ in New York for five years now.
(1) living
(2) lives
(3) has lived
(4) lived
= 3
~ 'Has lived' is correct because an action or state that started in the past and continues into the present, with a stated period of time, takes the present perfect (have/has + past participle). The time phrase 'for five years now' is the signal: 'She has lived in New York for five years now' means she moved there five years ago and still lives there. 'Has been living' (present perfect continuous) would also work, but it is not offered. 'Living' alone is a participle with no helping verb, so the sentence would have no finite verb. 'Lives' (simple present) states a present fact or habit but cannot be combined with 'for five years' in standard English; this is a common Indian-English error ('I am working here since 2020' should be 'I have been working here since 2020'). 'Lived' (simple past) means the action is over, which contradicts 'now'. Remember: 'for' + a period (for five years, for a decade) and 'since' + a point of time (since 2020, since Monday) both go with the perfect tenses when the action continues to the present.

Q 35 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
I will be visiting ______ Amazon rainforest next year.
(1) a
(2) an
(3) the
(4) no article
= 3
~ 'The' is used because the Amazon rainforest is a unique, specific geographical feature, and English uses the definite article with the names of rivers, oceans, seas, deserts, mountain ranges, groups of islands and great forests. So we say the Amazon, the Amazon rainforest, the Mahanadi, the Bay of Bengal, the Thar Desert, the Himalayas, the Andaman and Nicobar Islands and the Sundarbans. 'A' and 'an' are indefinite articles for any one of many unspecified things ('a forest', 'an island'); there is only one Amazon rainforest, so neither fits. 'No article' is wrong because the phrase names a particular natural feature, and such names need 'the'. Contrast: single mountains, single lakes, cities, states, countries and continents usually take no article (Mount Everest, Lake Victoria, Bhubaneswar, Odisha, India, Asia), except country names that are plural or contain words like Republic, Kingdom or States (the Netherlands, the United Kingdom, the United States). Tip: river, ocean, sea and desert names always take 'the'; a single peak usually does not (Mount Everest, Kanchenjunga). Another example: 'We sailed across the Indian Ocean.'

Q 36 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
If it rains tomorrow, I ______ my umbrella.
(1) will bring
(2) brings
(3) was bringing
(4) be bringing
= 1
~ 'Will bring' completes a first conditional, which talks about a real and likely situation in the future. Its pattern is: If + simple present, subject + will + base verb. The if-clause 'If it rains tomorrow' is already in the simple present (rains, not 'will rain'), so the main clause needs 'will bring'. A common error is to put 'will' in both clauses: 'If it will rain tomorrow' is wrong. 'Brings' is simple present with -s, which does not agree with 'I' and gives no future meaning. 'Was bringing' is past continuous and clashes with 'tomorrow'. 'Be bringing' lacks a helping verb; it would need 'will be bringing', which is possible but not offered. Compare the other conditionals: zero (general truth) 'If you heat ice, it melts'; second (unreal present) 'If it rained, I would bring my umbrella'; third (unreal past) 'If it had rained, I would have brought my umbrella.' Another first-conditional example: 'If you study daily, you will clear the exam.' Tip: in an if-clause about the future, do not use will.

Q 37 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
He has a ______ attitude towards his colleagues, making the workplace enjoyable.
(1) negative
(2) critical
(3) cheerful
(4) indifferent
= 3
~ 'Cheerful' means happy, positive and friendly, and only such an attitude could make 'the workplace enjoyable'. The participle phrase 'making the workplace enjoyable' describes the result of his attitude, so the blank must be a positive adjective; in vocabulary blanks, always check whether the clue words are positive or negative before choosing. 'Negative' means pessimistic or unfriendly, which would spoil the workplace, the opposite of the clue. 'Critical' means fault-finding or disapproving (it can also mean very important, as in 'a critical decision'), and colleagues rarely enjoy constant criticism. 'Indifferent' means showing no interest or concern; it would make the atmosphere cold, not enjoyable. Note the prepositions: an attitude towards (or to) people, and indifferent to something. Synonyms of cheerful: jovial, genial, buoyant, upbeat, amiable; antonyms: gloomy, morose, sullen. Another example: 'Her cheerful nature lifted everyone's spirits during the long election duty.' Trap: don't confuse 'indifferent' (uncaring) with 'different' (not the same), or with 'indifferent' in the sense of mediocre ('an indifferent performance').

Q 38 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
They ______ each other for over a decade when they finally met.
(1) have known
(2) had known
(3) know
(4) were knowing
= 2
~ 'Had known' is correct because the sentence describes a state that continued up to a particular moment in the past, and that is the job of the past perfect (had + past participle). The reference point is 'when they finally met', which is in the simple past. Their knowing each other, perhaps through letters or phone calls, came first and lasted until that meeting. Timeline: had known (earlier, ongoing) → met (later, past). 'Have known' is present perfect, which connects the past to the present moment, not to a past event; it would fit 'They have known each other for ten years now'. 'Know' is simple present and ignores the past setting. 'Were knowing' is wrong for two reasons: it is past continuous, and 'know' is a stative verb (a verb of mental state) that is not normally used in continuous forms, like understand, believe, own and belong. Another example: 'By the time the police arrived, the thief had escaped.' Tip: 'by the time', 'when' or 'before' + simple past often signals had + past participle in the other clause.

Q 39 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The weather was ______ yesterday, but today it feels quite chilly.
(1) warm
(2) cool
(3) cold
(4) rainy
= 1
~ 'Warm' is correct because the conjunction 'but' shows contrast, so yesterday's weather must be the opposite of today's 'quite chilly' (unpleasantly cold). Warm, meaning fairly or pleasantly hot, is the natural antonym of chilly. In contrast blanks, look at the connector first: but, although, yet, however and whereas signal an opposite idea, while and, also and moreover signal a similar idea. 'Cool' means slightly cold, which is close to chilly, so there is no real contrast. 'Cold' means almost the same as chilly, so 'It was cold yesterday, but today it feels quite chilly' is illogical. 'Rainy' describes rainfall, not temperature, so it does not form the hot–cold contrast the sentence sets up. A temperature scale to remember, from lowest to highest: freezing → cold → chilly → cool → mild → warm → hot → scorching. Another example: 'The morning was sunny, but the evening turned stormy.' Grammar note: 'feels' here is a linking verb, so it is followed by an adjective (feels chilly), not an adverb.

Q 40 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
She has a ______ interest in classical literature and often discusses it passionately.
(1) profound
(2) shallow
(3) casual
(4) limited
= 1
~ 'Profound' means very deep, intense or far-reaching, and a person who 'often discusses it passionately' clearly has a deep interest, so 'a profound interest in classical literature' fits. The clue words 'often' (frequently) and 'passionately' (with strong feeling) point to a strong, positive degree. 'Shallow' means superficial or lacking depth, the direct antonym of profound. 'Casual' means relaxed and not serious; a casual interest would not lead to passionate discussions. 'Limited' means small or restricted in extent, which also contradicts the clue. Profound collocates with words like knowledge, impact, effect, change, silence and respect: 'The film had a profound impact on young viewers.' Its noun is profundity. Synonyms: deep, keen, intense, abiding; antonyms: superficial, slight, cursory. Note the preposition too: an interest in something, and interested in, never 'interested on'. Tip: when three options mean 'small or weak' and one means 'strong', and the clue words are strong, choose the strong one.

Q 41 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The author's style is considered quite ______, often leaving readers enthralled.
(1) challenging
(2) engaging
(3) dull
(4) uninteresting
= 2
~ 'Engaging' means charming, interesting and able to hold attention, and that explains why the author's style is 'often leaving readers enthralled'. Enthralled means captivated or spellbound, so the style must be one that grips the reader; 'considered quite engaging' is a positive judgement that matches the positive result. 'Challenging' means difficult and demanding effort; a hard style might impress some readers, but difficulty alone does not explain why they are captivated, so it is not the best fit. 'Dull' means boring and lacking interest, the opposite of enthralling. 'Uninteresting' also means boring and contradicts 'enthralled'. Structure note: the participle phrase 'often leaving readers enthralled' shows the effect of the style, so the adjective must be one that causes that effect. Word family: engage (verb), engagement (noun), engaging (adjective), engaged (busy, or promised in marriage). Synonyms of engaging: captivating, absorbing, compelling, riveting; antonyms: tedious, monotonous, dreary. Another example: 'The teacher's engaging style kept the students attentive for the whole lecture.'

Q 42 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
She has ______ unique perspective on the issue.
(1) a
(2) an
(3) the
(4) none of the above
= 1
~ 'A' is correct because the choice between 'a' and 'an' depends on the first sound of the next word, not its first letter. 'Unique' is spelt with the vowel u, but it is pronounced /juː-/, like 'you', which begins with a consonant sound (y). So we say 'a unique perspective', just as we say a university, a uniform, a European country and a one-rupee coin (one begins with a 'w' sound). 'An' is used before vowel sounds: an umbrella, an honest officer, an hour, an MLA (M is pronounced 'em'). The indefinite article fits the meaning too: she has one distinctive viewpoint among many possible ones, introduced for the first time. 'The' would point to a specific perspective already known to the reader, which the sentence does not suggest (it would need extra information, such as 'the unique perspective that only a farmer has'). 'None of the above' would leave 'has unique perspective', which is ungrammatical because a singular countable noun needs an article. Tip: say the word aloud before choosing a or an.

Q 43 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The hypothesis was ______ untenable, collapsing under peer review.
(1) utterly
(2) uttering
(3) utter
(4) uttered
= 1
~ 'Utterly' is the adverb meaning completely or totally, and it is needed because the blank modifies the adjective 'untenable'. Rule: an adjective is modified by an adverb (utterly untenable, extremely clear, highly unlikely), not by another adjective or a verb form. Untenable means impossible to defend or maintain against criticism, which is why the hypothesis 'collapsed under peer review'. 'Utter' as an adjective also means complete, but it is used before a noun ('utter nonsense', 'an utter failure'), not before an adjective; as a verb, utter means to speak ('He did not utter a word'). 'Uttering' is the present participle of that verb and 'uttered' its past form; 'was uttering untenable' or 'was uttered untenable' make no sense. Utterly is usually used with negative or extreme words: utterly ridiculous, utterly exhausted, utterly destroyed. Synonyms: completely, entirely, wholly, totally. Another example: 'The witness's story proved utterly false.' Tip: if the word after the blank is an adjective, the blank usually needs an -ly adverb.

Q 44 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The initial experiment failed. ______, the team learned valuable lessons.
(1) However
(2) Sparingly
(3) Meticulously
(4) Since
= 1
~ 'However' is the right connector because the two sentences contrast: the experiment failed (negative), yet the team learned valuable lessons (positive). 'However' is a conjunctive adverb that introduces a contrasting idea in a new sentence, and it is followed by a comma: 'The initial experiment failed. However, the team learned valuable lessons.' 'Since' is a subordinating conjunction meaning 'because' or 'from a past time'; it needs two clauses ('Since it failed, we changed the method'), so 'Since, the team learned valuable lessons' is incomplete, and a cause sense would be illogical anyway. 'Sparingly' means using little of something ('Use water sparingly'), and 'Meticulously' means very carefully; both are adverbs of manner that describe how an action is done, not linkers between sentences. Other contrast connectors: nevertheless, nonetheless, yet, still, on the other hand. Addition connectors: moreover, furthermore, besides. Result connectors: therefore, hence, consequently. Punctuation tip: when however joins two full clauses in one sentence, put a semicolon before it: 'It rained; however, the match continued.'

Q 45 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The witness ______ having been near the scene that night.
(1) relied
(2) admitted
(3) expected
(4) failed
= 2
~ 'Admitted' is correct because 'admit' (to confess or accept that something is true, often unwillingly) is followed by a gerund, and 'having been' is the perfect gerund, which refers to an earlier action: 'The witness admitted having been near the scene that night.' The meaning also suits a courtroom or police context. Verbs that take a gerund include admit, deny, avoid, enjoy, consider, mind, regret, suggest and finish. 'Relied' needs the preposition 'on' and a noun ('relied on the evidence'); 'relied having been' is ungrammatical. 'Expected' is followed by a to-infinitive ('expected to be near the scene'), not a gerund. 'Failed' also takes a to-infinitive ('failed to appear') and cannot be followed by 'having been'. Note that 'admit to' + gerund is also correct: 'He admitted to having taken the money.' The same pattern appears with deny: 'The accused denied having seen the victim.' Tip: when you see 'having + past participle' right after the blank, look for a verb of the admit, deny or regret type.

Q 46 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
A group of students ______ planning to go to the beach now.
(1) be
(2) were
(3) are
(4) is
= 4
~ 'Is' fits because the head of the subject is 'group', a singular collective noun; 'of students' is only a phrase describing the group, and the verb agrees with the head noun, not with the nearest noun. The word 'now' shows an action in progress, so the present continuous (is + planning) is needed: 'A group of students is planning to go to the beach now.' 'Be' cannot stand alone after a subject in a statement; it needs a modal (will be planning). 'Were' is past tense and plural, so it clashes with both 'now' and the singular subject. 'Are' is plural; in British English a collective noun can take a plural verb when members act individually, and many speakers say 'A group of students are planning', but exam grammar treats 'a group of + plural noun' acting as one unit as singular, and the official key follows this. Similar examples: 'A bunch of keys is on the table', 'The committee has approved the plan', 'A team of doctors is examining the patient.' Trap: don't be pulled by the plural noun just before the verb.

Q 47 | eng | Vocabulary
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
He deeply ______ for not visiting his grandfather before he passed away.
(1) avoided
(2) regretted
(3) denied
(4) finished
= 2
~ 'Regretted' is correct because 'regret' means to feel sorry, sad or disappointed about something you did or failed to do, and 'deeply regretted' is a common collocation. The sentence says he did not visit his grandfather before the grandfather died, a loss he cannot undo, so sorrow is the natural feeling. Grammar note: in careful English, regret takes the gerund directly, without 'for': 'He deeply regretted not visiting his grandfather.' Also learn the difference between 'regret doing' (sorry about a past action) and 'regret to inform/say' (sorry about news you are giving now: 'We regret to inform you that the exam is postponed'). 'Avoided' means kept away from something on purpose; 'deeply avoided for not visiting' makes no sense. 'Denied' means refused to admit or refused to give, which is the opposite of owning up and feeling sorry. 'Finished' means completed and has no link to sorrow. Synonyms: repented, lamented, rued; related nouns: regret, remorse. 'Passed away' is a polite way of saying died.

Q 48 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
She usually ______ breakfast at 7 AM.
(1) eat
(2) eats
(3) eating
(4) has eaten
= 2
~ 'Eats' is correct because 'usually' is an adverb of frequency showing a habit or routine, and habits are expressed in the simple present tense. With a third-person singular subject (he, she, it, or one person's name), the simple present verb takes -s or -es: she eats, he goes, it rains. So: 'She usually eats breakfast at 7 AM.' 'Eat' is the base form, used with I, you, we and they ('They usually eat at 7'). 'Eating' is a participle and needs a helping verb ('She is eating'); besides, the present continuous is for actions happening now, not routines. 'Has eaten' is present perfect, which describes a completed action with a present result ('She has eaten, so she is not hungry'); it does not suit a daily habit at a fixed clock time. Other frequency adverbs that signal the simple present: always, often, generally, sometimes, rarely, never, every day. Position tip: these adverbs come before the main verb (usually eats) but after 'be' (She is usually early). Another example: 'The office usually opens at 10 AM.'

Q 49 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
If I ______ you, I would have seized that opportunity immediately.
(1) am
(2) were
(3) was
(4) have been
= 2
~ 'Were' is correct because 'If I were you' is the standard form for an imaginary or impossible condition: the speaker can never actually be the other person. This is the subjunctive mood, in which 'were' is used with every subject, including I, he, she and it ('If he were rich, he would help'). The main clause 'I would have seized that opportunity immediately' refers to a past chance, so the full sentence is a mixed conditional: an unreal present identity (If I were you) with an unreal past result (would have seized). 'If I had been you' would also be possible, but it is not offered. 'Am' expresses a real, possible condition ('If I am late, start without me'), which cannot apply here. 'Was' is heard in informal speech, but formal and exam English prefers 'were' for unreal conditions. 'Have been' does not form a standard conditional after 'if' with 'would have'. Other uses of the subjunctive were: 'I wish I were taller'; 'He talks as if he were the boss.' Tip: imaginary 'if' or 'wish' with the verb be → were.

Q 50 | eng | Grammar
Directions : Fill in the blanks in the following sentences by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
Neither of the candidates ______ very experienced in this field.
(1) is
(2) are
(3) be
(4) were
= 1
~ 'Is' is correct because 'neither of' + plural noun refers to each of the two candidates separately (not this one and not that one), so in standard and exam English it takes a singular verb: 'Neither of the candidates is very experienced in this field.' The same rule applies to either of, each of, one of and every one of: 'Each of the boys has a bag', 'One of my friends lives in Cuttack.' The plural noun 'candidates' after 'of' is a distractor; the verb agrees with 'neither'. 'Are' is plural; it is common in informal speech, but exam keys treat it as wrong. 'Be' cannot be the main verb of a statement without a modal. 'Were' is plural and past; with a singular subject it appears only in unreal conditions (If I were ...). Contrast 'neither of' with 'neither ... nor', where the verb agrees with the nearer subject: 'Neither the manager nor the clerks were present', but 'Neither the clerks nor the manager was present.' Also note: neither is used for two people or things; for more than two, use none.

Q 51 | eng | Grammar
______ had such data been accessible to researchers outside elite institutions.
(1) Never before
(2) Lefore never
(3) Heretofore
(4) Hitherto
= 1
~ 'Never before' is correct because a negative adverbial at the start of a clause causes subject–auxiliary inversion, and the sentence already shows that inversion: 'had such data been', with the auxiliary 'had' placed before the subject 'such data'. Normal order would be 'Such data had never before been accessible to researchers outside elite institutions.' Moving 'Never before' to the front for emphasis gives 'Never before had such data been accessible ...', meaning this is the first time such data is available to them. 'Heretofore' and 'Hitherto' both mean 'until now' or 'up to this time', but they are not negative words, so they do not trigger inversion, and 'Hitherto had such data been accessible' would say the opposite of the intended meaning. 'Lefore never' is a misprint, and even 'Before never' is not valid English; the correct order is 'never before'. Other negative or restrictive starters that invert: Seldom, Rarely, Hardly ... when, No sooner ... than, Not only ... but also, Under no circumstances, Only then. Example: 'Seldom have I seen such dedication.'

Q 52 | eng | Grammar
The data was analysed ______, yielding insights that revolutionized the field.
(1) exhaustively
(2) exhaustive
(3) exhaustion
(4) in exhaust
= 1
~ 'Exhaustively' is correct because the blank describes how the data 'was analysed', and a word that modifies a verb must be an adverb. Exhaustively means thoroughly and completely, covering every possible detail, which explains why the analysis was 'yielding insights that revolutionized the field'. 'Exhaustive' is the adjective form; it describes a noun ('an exhaustive study', 'an exhaustive list'), so 'analysed exhaustive' is ungrammatical. 'Exhaustion' is a noun meaning extreme tiredness or the complete using up of something (exhaustion of resources), so it fits neither the grammar nor the meaning. 'In exhaust' is not an English phrase; exhaust as a noun means waste gases from an engine. Word family: exhaust (verb: to tire out or use up completely), exhausted (very tired), exhausting (very tiring), exhaustive (thorough), exhaustively (thoroughly). Trap: exhaustive ≠ exhausting; an exhaustive report is complete, while an exhausting day makes you tired. Another example: 'The committee examined the files exhaustively before submitting its report.' Note: 'data' commonly takes a singular verb (was) in modern usage.

Q 53 | eng | Grammar
The study focuses ______ the relationship between diet and mental health.
(1) in
(2) at
(3) on
(4) with
= 3
~ 'Focus on' is the fixed verb–preposition combination, so 'The study focuses on the relationship between diet and mental health' is correct. Focus means to concentrate attention or effort on one thing, and both the verb and the noun take 'on': focus on your studies, the focus on rural health, a sharp focus on results. Such dependent prepositions have to be learned with the word, because no simple logic predicts them. 'In' goes with words like interest, belief or involvement (interested in, believe in, involved in), not with focus. 'At' goes with points and targets (aim at, look at, arrive at), but 'focuses at' is not standard. 'With' means 'together with' (agree with, deal with) and does not express concentration. Similar verbs that take 'on': concentrate on, depend on, rely on, insist on, comment on, reflect on, dwell on. Exam tip: if a sentence is about attention or emphasis (focus, concentrate, emphasis, stress), 'on' is almost always the preposition. Another example: 'Odisha's Mission Shakti focuses on women's self-help groups.'

Q 54 | eng | Vocabulary
She was feeling quite ______ after working late into the night.
(1) content
(2) exhausted
(3) energetic
(4) happy
= 2
~ 'Exhausted' means extremely tired, completely drained of energy, and that is the natural result of 'working late into the night'. In cause-and-effect sentences, the reason given tells you which feeling to choose; here the word 'after' links the cause (late work) to the effect (tiredness). 'Content' means satisfied or at peace; it does not follow from the tiring cause, and 'quite content' says nothing about fatigue. 'Energetic' means full of energy, the opposite of what late-night work produces. 'Happy' is a general positive feeling with no link to the cause. Word family: exhaust (verb), exhaustion (noun), exhausting (causing tiredness), exhausted (feeling tired). Trap: -ing adjectives describe the cause, -ed adjectives describe the person's feeling: 'The work was exhausting; she was exhausted.' The same pattern works for boring/bored, tiring/tired and interesting/interested. Note also the stress in content: CON-tent is the noun (what something contains), con-TENT the adjective (satisfied). Synonyms of exhausted: worn out, drained, fatigued, weary.

Q 55 | eng | Grammar
The youngest runner finished the race ______, surprising all the coaches.
(1) quick
(2) quicker
(3) quickly
(4) quickness
= 3
~ 'Quickly' is correct because the blank tells us how the runner finished the race, and a word that modifies a verb (finished) must be an adverb. The verb already has its object, 'the race', and the adverb of manner comes after it: 'The youngest runner finished the race quickly, surprising all the coaches.' 'Quick' is an adjective that describes a noun ('a quick runner', 'a quick reply'); people say 'Come quick!' informally, but in written exam English the -ly adverb is required here. 'Quicker' is a comparative form; it would need a comparison with 'than' ('quicker than the others'), which the sentence lacks, and in formal English the comparative adverb is 'more quickly'. 'Quickness' is a noun meaning speed, which cannot describe how an action is done unless a preposition is added ('with quickness'). Also note 'youngest', a superlative used because the runner is compared with all the others. Tip: ask 'how?' about the verb; if the answer fills the blank, choose the adverb. Another example: 'She solved the puzzle easily.'

Q 56 | eng | Vocabulary
After the bitter quarrel, he felt a sense of ______ for losing temper.
(1) lethargy
(2) remorse
(3) ennui
(4) apathy
= 2
~ 'Remorse' means deep regret or guilt for a wrong one has done, and it is exactly what a person feels after a bitter quarrel in which he lost his temper. The phrase 'a sense of ___ for losing temper' needs a feeling about one's own mistake, and remorse takes 'for' or 'over': remorse for his words, remorse over the incident. 'Lethargy' means sluggishness or lack of energy, a kind of tiredness, not guilt. 'Ennui' (a French word, pronounced on-WEE) means boredom or listlessness caused by having nothing interesting to do. 'Apathy' means lack of interest, feeling or concern; an apathetic person would not care about the quarrel at all, which contradicts the guilt implied. Synonyms of remorse: repentance, contrition, penitence, compunction; adjective: remorseful; and remorseless means cruel, without pity. Trap: regret can be mild disappointment ('I regret missing the bus'), while remorse always involves moral guilt. Grammar note: the standard idiom is 'losing his temper', with a possessive. Another example: 'The accused showed no remorse in court.'

Q 57 | eng | Vocabulary
His performance was considered ______ compared to the others, who excelled.
(1) brilliant
(2) exceptional
(3) impressive
(4) mediocre
= 4
~ 'Mediocre' means only average or ordinary, not very good, and it fits because the sentence contrasts his performance with that of 'the others, who excelled'. 'Compared to' sets up a comparison, and the clause 'who excelled' (did extremely well) tells us the others were outstanding, so his performance must have fallen short. 'Brilliant', 'exceptional' and 'impressive' are all words of high praise; any of them would remove the contrast and make the comparison pointless. Look at the option pattern: three options share one meaning (very good) and only one differs; when the context demands contrast, the odd word is usually the answer. Mediocre comes from a Latin word that literally meant 'halfway up a mountain', a neat way to remember 'middling'. Noun: mediocrity. Synonyms: average, ordinary, middling, run-of-the-mill, so-so, lacklustre; antonyms: outstanding, excellent, superb. Usage note: 'compared to' and 'compared with' are both accepted in modern English. Another example: 'The film received mediocre reviews despite its big budget.'

Q 58 | eng | Vocabulary
Despite the harsh weather, the team remained ______ in their effort to complete the project.
(1) fearful
(2) indifferent
(3) pessimistic
(4) optimistic
= 4
~ 'Optimistic' means hopeful and confident about the future, and it fits because 'despite' signals a contrast: in spite of harsh weather, which would normally discourage people, the team stayed positive. 'Remained' is a linking verb, so it is followed by an adjective describing the team's state ('remained optimistic'). 'Fearful' (afraid) and 'pessimistic' (expecting the worst) are the feelings harsh weather would naturally cause, so using them after 'despite' destroys the contrast; 'despite' needs an outcome that goes against the expected one. 'Indifferent' means uncaring or uninterested, which clashes with 'in their effort to complete the project', since an indifferent team would make little effort. Contrast markers to watch: despite, in spite of, although, though, even though, nevertheless. Grammar note: 'despite' takes a noun or gerund directly ('despite the weather', 'despite working hard'), never 'despite of'; 'in spite of' needs 'of'. Synonyms of optimistic: hopeful, upbeat, positive, sanguine; antonyms: pessimistic, gloomy, despondent. Another example: 'Despite the loss, the captain remained optimistic.'

Q 59 | eng | Grammar
She had asked them ______ interrupt her during the presentation.
(1) not
(2) don't
(3) not to
(4) will
= 3
~ 'Not to' is correct because 'ask' in reported requests follows the pattern ask + object + to-infinitive, and the negative puts 'not' before 'to': ask someone not to do something. So 'She had asked them not to interrupt her during the presentation.' The direct speech would be 'Please don't interrupt me.' In reported speech, 'don't' becomes 'not to', and 'me' becomes 'her'. 'Not' alone gives 'asked them not interrupt', which lacks the infinitive marker 'to'. 'Don't' is a contracted imperative used only in direct speech; it cannot follow 'asked them'. 'Will' is a modal and gives 'asked them will interrupt', which is ungrammatical. Other verbs with the same pattern: tell, request, order, advise, warn, urge, remind, forbid: 'The teacher warned the students not to copy.' Watch the position of 'not': 'not to go' is the standard order; 'to not go' (a split infinitive) is common in speech but best avoided in exam English. Tip: request or command + object → to-infinitive; negative → not to.

Q 60 | eng | Grammar
He spoke ______, avoiding any terminology that might escalate tensions.
(1) measured
(2) measuredly
(3) in a measuring way
(4) with measure
= 2
~ 'Measuredly' is the adverb form of the adjective 'measured', and the blank needs an adverb because it describes how he spoke (it modifies the verb 'spoke'). Measured here means careful, controlled and deliberate, so 'He spoke measuredly, avoiding any terminology that might escalate tensions' means he chose his words with care to keep things calm. The word is rare, but it is a genuine dictionary word. 'Measured' is an adjective and normally describes a noun ('a measured response', 'measured steps', 'a measured tone'); 'He spoke measured' is ungrammatical. 'In a measuring way' wrongly uses the present participle 'measuring' (taking measurements), which changes the meaning. 'With measure' is not idiomatic; the noun measure appears in phrases such as 'beyond measure' (extremely) and 'in large measure' (to a great extent), not for a manner of speaking. In everyday writing, the same idea is usually expressed as 'He spoke in a measured tone'. Related words: escalate (to increase or intensify), de-escalate (to calm down). Tip: verb + how? → adverb, even when the adverb sounds unusual.

Q 61 | eng | Vocabulary
The team's ______ was evident in the way they played together and won.
(1) difference
(2) unity
(3) discord
(4) cacophony
= 2
~ 'Unity' means oneness or togetherness, the state of working as a single, united group, and 'the way they played together and won' shows exactly that. The word 'evident' (clearly visible) tells us the blank names a quality that their play revealed, and playing together successfully reveals unity. 'Difference' means dissimilarity or a disagreement; it does not explain why they played together and won. 'Discord' means conflict or lack of agreement among people, the opposite of unity. 'Cacophony' means a harsh, unpleasant mixture of sounds (from Greek, 'bad sound'); it is used for noise, such as 'a cacophony of horns in traffic', and only figuratively for clashing opinions, so it does not fit a winning team. Synonyms of unity: solidarity, harmony, cohesion, team spirit, concord; antonyms: discord, division, disunity, strife. Word family: unite (verb), united (adjective), unity (noun); the root uni- means 'one', as in uniform and unanimous (all agreeing). Pair to remember: euphony (pleasant sound) is the opposite of cacophony. Another example: 'The unity of the villagers helped them rebuild after the cyclone.'

Q 62 | eng | Vocabulary
We are unhappy because the results of the experiment are ______ to our expectations.
(1) contrary
(2) consistent
(3) supportive
(4) expected
= 1
~ 'Contrary' fits because 'contrary to' means opposite to or against, and results that go against our expectations would make us unhappy, as the sentence says. 'Contrary to' is a fixed phrase: contrary to expectations, contrary to popular belief, contrary to the rules. The word 'because' links the cause (results against expectations) to the effect (we are unhappy), so the blank must carry a negative sense. 'Consistent' means matching or in agreement, and it takes 'with', not 'to' ('consistent with our expectations'); matching results would please us, not upset us. 'Supportive' means giving help or encouragement and is normally used for people ('supportive of the plan'); results that support our idea would also please us, and 'supportive to our expectations' is unidiomatic. 'Expected' creates a meaningless repetition: 'expected to our expectations'. Synonyms of contrary: opposite, conflicting, contradictory, opposed. The phrase 'on the contrary' means 'the opposite is true' and starts a correction: 'He is not lazy; on the contrary, he works very hard.' Tip: always test the preposition that follows the blank.

Q 63 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
______, the data strongly contradicts the initial hypothesis.
(1) No sooner does
(2) Summary
(3) In as much as
(4) Significantly
= 4
~ 'Significantly' is a sentence adverb: placed at the start and followed by a comma, it comments on the whole statement and means 'importantly' or 'in a way that deserves attention'. So 'Significantly, the data strongly contradicts the initial hypothesis' tells the reader that this finding is noteworthy. Other sentence adverbs used the same way: interestingly, surprisingly, clearly, fortunately, notably. 'No sooner does' begins an inverted structure that must be completed by 'than' and a second clause ('No sooner had the data arrived than it was challenged'); here there is no 'than', and a comma cannot follow 'does'. 'Summary' is a noun; to introduce a conclusion you need a phrase such as 'In summary' or 'To sum up'. 'In as much as' (usually written 'inasmuch as') is a conjunction meaning 'since' or 'to the extent that', and it must be followed by a clause ('Inasmuch as the data is reliable, we must revise the theory'), not by a comma. Note also 'data contradicts': in modern usage data often takes a singular verb, though formal science writing may use 'data contradict'. Tip: a single -ly word plus a comma at the start is usually a sentence adverb.

Q 64 | eng | Grammar
The collection of rare manuscripts ______ housed in the climate-controlled archive.
(1) are
(2) were
(3) have been
(4) is
= 4
~ 'Is' is correct because the head noun of the subject is 'collection', which is singular; 'of rare manuscripts' is a prepositional phrase that only describes the collection, so it does not decide the verb. The sentence is in the present passive (is + past participle 'housed'): 'The collection of rare manuscripts is housed in the climate-controlled archive.'
