'Are' and 'were' are plural forms, picked by students who wrongly match the verb with the nearest noun, 'manuscripts'; 'were' is also past tense. 'Have been' is plural as well; the singular would be 'has been housed', which is not offered. This trap is called the proximity error: the verb must agree with the true subject, not with the noun closest to it. Other examples: 'The box of chocolates is on the table', 'The quality of these mangoes is excellent', 'A bouquet of roses was delivered.' Nouns such as collection, bunch, set, series and pair, when followed by 'of + plural noun', usually take a singular verb. Tip: cover the 'of' phrase with your finger and read 'The collection ___ housed'; the answer becomes obvious.

Q 65 | eng | Grammar
Directions: Identify the incorrect segment in the sentences. Select the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
Not only [does] the rain ruin the picnic, [but also] the wind made [it impossible] to set up the tent [in the slope].
(1) does
(2) but also
(3) it impossible
(4) in the slope
= 1
~ 'Does' is the error because the sentence is about a past event: the second clause uses the past tense ('the wind made it impossible'), so the first clause must also be in the past. The corrected sentence is 'Not only did the rain ruin the picnic, but also the wind made it impossible to set up the tent on the slope.' When 'Not only' opens a sentence, the auxiliary comes before the subject (inversion), as in a question: not only did the rain ruin, not only has he lied, not only is she clever. The inversion itself is right; only the present-tense 'does' clashes with 'made'. 'But also' is the correct partner of 'not only'. 'It impossible' is correct, with 'it' as a dummy object followed by the adjective and the to-infinitive (made it impossible to set up). 'In the slope' is unidiomatic, since we normally say 'on the slope' for a surface, but OPSC's key marks the tense error, which is the main grammatical fault. Rule to remember: keep tenses consistent in paired clauses. Another example: 'Not only did he win the match, but he also broke the record.'

Q 66 | eng | Grammar
Directions: Identify the incorrect segment in the sentences. Select the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
Neither the manager [nor] his assistant [were] aware of the new regulations, and this [caused] significant confusion [among] the staff.”
(1) nor
(2) were
(3) caused
(4) among
= 2
~ 'Were' is the error because, with the pair 'neither ... nor' (and 'either ... or'), the verb agrees with the subject closest to it. Here the nearer subject is 'his assistant', which is singular, so the verb must be 'was': 'Neither the manager nor his assistant was aware of the new regulations, and this caused significant confusion among the staff.' This is called the rule of proximity. If the nearer subject were plural, the verb would be plural: 'Neither the manager nor his assistants were aware.' Tip: when one subject is singular and one plural, put the plural one second so that the plural verb sounds natural. 'Nor' is correct, since neither always pairs with nor (not 'or'). 'Caused' is correct: it is past tense, matching the past setting, and its subject 'this' refers to the whole situation. 'Among' is correct because the staff are more than two people; 'between' is used mainly for two. Contrast 'neither of them', which takes a singular verb in exam English ('Neither of them was aware'). Similarly: 'Either the clerks or the officer has the file.'

Q 67 | eng | Grammar
Directions: Identify the incorrect segment in the sentences. Select the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
[If] I had [been] rich in my youth, I [will] have [bought] a car
(1) If
(2) been
(3) will
(4) bought
= 3
~ 'Will' is the error because this is a third conditional, which talks about an imaginary past situation that did not happen. Its pattern is: If + past perfect (had + past participle), subject + would have + past participle. The if-clause is already correct, 'If I had been rich in my youth' (in fact, I was not rich), so the result clause must be 'I would have bought a car' (in fact, I did not buy one). 'Will have bought' is the future perfect, which describes something completed before a future time ('By next year I will have bought a car'); it cannot express an unreal past result. 'If' is correct as the conditional conjunction. 'Been' is correct as the past participle of 'be' in 'had been'. 'Bought' is the correct past participle of buy (buy–bought–bought), so 'would have bought' is right once 'will' is changed. Summary of conditionals: first, If it rains, I will stay; second, If it rained, I would stay; third, If it had rained, I would have stayed. Trap: don't put 'would' inside the if-clause itself; 'If I would have been rich' is wrong.

Q 68 | eng | Grammar
Directions: Identify the incorrect segment in the sentences. Select the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
Everyone [feel] a great [sense of] elation [when] one [gets] a dream job.
(1) feel
(2) sense of
(3) when
(4) gets
= 1
~ 'Feel' is the error because 'everyone' is a singular indefinite pronoun, and in the simple present a singular subject takes a verb with -s: 'Everyone feels a great sense of elation when one gets a dream job.' Although 'everyone' means all people, grammatically it refers to each person individually, so it behaves like 'he' or 'she'. The same rule applies to everybody, everything, someone, somebody, anyone, nobody, no one, each and every: 'Each of the students has a locker', 'Nobody knows the answer.' 'Sense of' is correct, as 'a sense of' is the standard phrase for a feeling (a sense of pride, a sense of relief, a sense of duty). 'When' is correct as a time conjunction. 'Gets' is correct because 'one' is singular. Elation means great happiness and excitement; its adjective is elated. Note: in careful English, 'everyone ... when they get' or 'when he or she gets' is more usual than switching to 'one', but the sentence's real grammatical error is the verb 'feel'. Trap: the meaning 'all people' tempts students to use a plural verb, but the grammar is singular.

Q 69 | eng | Grammar
Directions: Identify the incorrect segment in the sentences. Select the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
Immigration rules [has become] so stringent [that] visa verification [happens] at multiple levels before a passenger [is given] entry.
(1) has become
(2) that
(3) happens
(4) is given
= 1
~ 'Has become' is the error because the subject, 'Immigration rules', is plural (rules), so it needs the plural helping verb 'have': 'Immigration rules have become so stringent that visa verification happens at multiple levels before a passenger is given entry.' In the present perfect, has goes with singular subjects (he, she, it, a rule) and have with plural ones (they, rules). The word 'immigration' only describes the rules, like an adjective; it is not the head of the subject, so it should not make you choose the singular verb. 'That' is correct, completing the result pattern 'so + adjective + that' (so stringent that ...). 'Happens' is correct because its subject, 'visa verification', is singular. 'Is given' is correct, as 'a passenger' is singular and the passive is needed (the passenger receives entry). Stringent means strict and exacting, often used for rules, laws and standards. Similar examples: 'The new safety norms have made factories safer', 'The exam rules have been tightened.' Tip: find the main noun of the subject (rules), not the first noun you see (immigration).

Q 70 | eng | Grammar
Directions: Identify the incorrect segment in the sentences. Select the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The committee, which [has reached] a decision, [are] now preparing [their] official report [for publication].
(1) which
(2) has reached
(3) are
(4) their
= 3
~ 'Are' is the error because the collective noun 'committee' is acting as one single body here, as the singular verb in the relative clause, 'which has reached a decision', shows. A sentence must treat the same subject consistently, so the main verb should also be singular: 'The committee, which has reached a decision, is now preparing its official report for publication.' Rule: a collective noun (committee, jury, team, family, government) takes a singular verb when the group acts as a unit, and a plural verb when its members act separately or disagree ('The jury were divided in their opinions'). Here the members act together to prepare one report, so singular is right. 'Which' is correct, as it is the relative pronoun for things and groups, set off by commas in a non-defining clause. 'Has reached' is correct and is the clue to the singular use. Strictly, 'their' should also become 'its' for full consistency, but the verb is the main error the question targets, and the key marks 'are'. 'For publication' is fine. Another example: 'The government has announced its new policy.'

Q 71 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) the team
(Q) won the championship
(R) when they
(S) was ecstatic
(1) PSRQ
(2) RPSQ
(3) SQRP
(4) SPQR
= 1
~ PSRQ gives the sentence 'The team was ecstatic when they won the championship.' Start by finding the subject: 'the team' (P) is the only part that can open the sentence as a subject. It needs a main verb, and 'was ecstatic' (S) supplies one, so 'The team was ecstatic' is a complete main clause. 'When they' (R) begins a time clause and must be completed by its verb and object, 'won the championship' (Q). Ecstatic means overjoyed, extremely happy. Checking the other options: RPSQ gives 'When they the team was ecstatic won the championship', which is jumbled; SQRP gives 'Was ecstatic won the championship when they the team', which has no subject at the start; SPQR gives 'Was ecstatic the team won the championship when they', which also starts with a verb and ends with an unfinished clause. Note: the main clause treats the team as one unit (was), while 'they' refers to its members; this mixing is common and accepted. The order 'When they won the championship, the team was ecstatic' would also be correct, but it is not offered. Tip: in rearrangement questions, fix the subject + verb pair first, then attach the clauses.

Q 72 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) she
(Q) enjoys hiking
(R) with her friends
(S) and partying
(1) PSQR
(2) PQSR
(3) QPRS
(4) PRSQ
= 2
~ PQSR gives 'She enjoys hiking and partying with her friends.' The subject 'she' (P) comes first, then the verb with its first object, 'enjoys hiking' (Q). 'Enjoy' is a verb followed by a gerund (an -ing form used as a noun), so the second activity must also be a gerund, joined by 'and': 'and partying' (S). This is parallel structure: items joined by and, or or but should have the same grammatical form (hiking and partying, not 'hiking and to party'). Finally, 'with her friends' (R) tells us with whom she does both activities. Checking the others: PSQR gives 'She and partying enjoys hiking with her friends', which makes 'partying' a second subject and is absurd; QPRS begins with the verb, 'Enjoys hiking she with her friends and partying', with no subject in front; PRSQ gives 'She with her friends and partying enjoys hiking', which is muddled. Other verbs followed by gerunds: avoid, finish, mind, practise, suggest, consider, keep. Another example: 'He enjoys reading and writing poetry.' Tip: after placing the subject and verb, check that the joined items are parallel.

Q 73 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) last semester
(Q) I took
(R) a course
(S) on environmental science
(1) QPSR
(2) RPSQ
(3) PQRS
(4) PRSQ
= 3
~ PQRS gives 'Last semester I took a course on environmental science.' The time phrase 'last semester' (P) opens the sentence; time expressions can stand at the beginning or the end of a sentence. Then comes the subject + verb, 'I took' (Q), followed by its object, 'a course' (R), and finally the phrase 'on environmental science' (S), which tells us the topic of the course. A course on (or in) a subject is the standard collocation; 'took a course' means enrolled in and studied it, and other verbs that go with course are do, attend, complete and enrol in. Checking the others: QPSR gives 'I took last semester on environmental science a course', which splits the verb from its object; RPSQ gives 'A course last semester on environmental science I took', a jumbled order; PRSQ gives 'Last semester a course on environmental science I took', which puts the object before the subject and verb, a poetic order that is not normal English. Basic English word order is Subject–Verb–Object (SVO), with time phrases at either end. The order QRSP ('I took a course on environmental science last semester') would also be correct, but it is not offered.

Q 74 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) modern technology
(Q) the complexities of
(R) understands
(S) not everyone
(1) SRQP
(2) PRSQ
(3) SQPR
(4) RPQS
= 1
~ SRQP gives 'Not everyone understands the complexities of modern technology.' The subject is 'not everyone' (S), a negative form meaning 'some people do not'. Since everyone is grammatically singular, the verb 'understands' (R), with -s, follows it naturally; this is also a clue, because no plural subject is available. Next comes the object 'the complexities of' (Q), and the preposition 'of' must be followed by its noun phrase, 'modern technology' (P). Checking the others: PRSQ gives 'Modern technology understands not everyone the complexities of', which makes technology the subject and leaves 'of' hanging; SQPR gives 'Not everyone the complexities of modern technology understands', which puts the object before the verb; RPQS begins with the verb ('Understands modern technology the complexities of not everyone') and has no subject in front. Note the meaning of 'not everyone': it is partial negation (some do, some do not), unlike 'no one', which means nobody at all. Complexities means complicated aspects or details. Tip: a part ending with 'of' or another preposition must be followed directly by a noun part, a quick way to fix pairs such as QP.

Q 75 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) their dinner
(Q) went to the park
(R) after finishing
(S) her friends
(1) RSQP
(2) ASPR
(3) RPSQ
(4) PQRS
= 3
~ RPSQ gives 'After finishing their dinner, her friends went to the park.' The phrase 'after finishing' (R) must be followed by the object of 'finishing', which is 'their dinner' (P); together they form an introductory phrase that tells us when the action happened. The main clause follows: the subject 'her friends' (S) and the verb phrase 'went to the park' (Q). Note the agreement: 'their' (plural) matches 'her friends', and the people who finished dinner are the same people who went to the park, so there is no dangling modifier. Checking the others: RSQP gives 'After finishing her friends went to the park their dinner', which leaves 'their dinner' stranded at the end; PQRS gives 'Their dinner went to the park after finishing her friends', which makes the dinner the subject and is absurd; option B, 'ASPR', contains the letter A, which is not one of the parts, so it is a misprint and cannot be right. Trap to avoid: a dangling modifier such as 'After finishing dinner, the park was visited' wrongly suggests that the park finished dinner. Another example: 'After reading the notice, the students left the hall.'

Q 76 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) after five years of research
(Q) by her
(R) was discovered
(S) the cure
(1) SPQR
(2) PQRS
(3) QPRS
(4) SRQP
= 4
~ SRQP gives the passive sentence 'The cure was discovered by her after five years of research.' In the passive voice, the object of the active sentence becomes the subject: active 'She discovered the cure after five years of research' becomes passive 'The cure (S) was discovered (R) by her (Q) after five years of research (P).' The pattern is subject + be + past participle + by + agent, and phrases of time usually come at the end. Checking the others: SPQR gives 'The cure after five years of research by her was discovered', which separates the subject from its verb with a long phrase and sounds unnatural; PQRS gives 'After five years of research by her was discovered the cure', which puts the verb before its subject and reads as if 'by her' describes the research; QPRS gives 'By her after five years of research was discovered the cure', which is jumbled. The passive is used when the result (the cure) matters more than the doer. Note that the agent pronoun after 'by' is in the object form: by her, by him, by them (never 'by she'). Tip: a part starting with 'by' usually follows the passive verb.

Q 77 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) motivated the team
(Q) to enhance their performance
(R) by their coach
(S) the talk
(1) PRQS
(2) SRPQ
(3) PQRS
(4) SQRP
= 2
~ SRPQ gives 'The talk by their coach motivated the team to enhance their performance.' The subject is the noun phrase 'the talk' (S) with its modifier 'by their coach' (R), which tells us whose talk it was. The main verb and its object follow: 'motivated the team' (P). 'Motivate' takes the pattern motivate + object + to-infinitive, so 'to enhance their performance' (Q) comes last and states the result. Enhance means to improve or increase the quality of something. Checking the others: PRQS gives 'Motivated the team by their coach to enhance their performance the talk', which starts with a verb and has no subject in front; PQRS gives 'Motivated the team to enhance their performance by their coach the talk', again with no subject at the start; SQRP gives 'The talk to enhance their performance by their coach motivated the team', which separates 'the talk' from its modifier and muddles the meaning. Other verbs with the object + to-infinitive pattern: encourage, inspire, persuade, allow, enable, urge. Another example: 'The teacher encouraged the students to read newspapers daily.' Tip: when only one part can be a subject, start with it and then look for its verb.

Q 78 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) a vivid description
(Q) painted
(R) the author
(S) of the scene
(1) RSQP
(2) RQPS
(3) PRSQ
(4) RPSQ
= 2
~ RQPS gives the sentence 'The author painted a vivid description of the scene.' English follows the order Subject + Verb + Object: R 'the author' is the subject, Q 'painted' is the verb, P 'a vivid description' is the object, and S 'of the scene' is a prepositional phrase that must sit right after the noun it describes ('description of the scene'). Option A (RSQP) gives 'The author of the scene painted a vivid description', which attaches 'of the scene' to the author and changes the meaning, since a scene has no author. Option C (PRSQ) starts with the object and ends with the verb, which is not normal English order. Option D (RPSQ) puts the verb at the very end ('the author a vivid description of the scene painted'). That is the Subject–Object–Verb order of Odia and Hindi, a common trap for students who translate in their head. Quick method: first find the verb (here 'painted'), put the doer before it and the thing done after it, then attach each 'of' phrase to the noun it belongs to. One more example: 'The guide gave a clear account of the battle.'

Q 79 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) at the conference
(Q) experts will discuss
(R) the recent findings
(S) on climate change
(1) QRSP
(2) QSPR
(3) QPSR
(4) RPQS
= 1
~ QRSP gives 'Experts will discuss the recent findings on climate change at the conference.' Q is the subject and verb ('experts will discuss'), R is the object ('the recent findings'), S tells us which findings ('on climate change') and so must follow 'findings' directly, and P, the place phrase ('at the conference'), comes last. The key grammar point is that 'discuss' is a transitive verb: it takes its object directly, never 'discuss on' or 'discuss about'. Option B (QSPR) produces 'discuss on climate change at the conference the recent findings', which commits exactly that error and also cuts 'findings' off from the phrase that describes it. Option C (QPSR) wedges the place phrase between the verb and its object. Option D (RPQS) begins with the object and leaves 'on climate change' dangling at the end. Standard word order: Subject + Verb + Object + Place + Time. Similar examples: 'We discussed the budget at the meeting'; 'The committee will discuss the report tomorrow.' Remember the verbs that take no preposition before their object: discuss, describe, reach, resemble, accompany.

Q 80 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) to travel abroad
(Q) he decided
(R) for the treatment
(S) of his son
(1) PSQR
(2) RPQS
(3) QPRS
(4) PRSQ
= 3
~ QPRS gives 'He decided to travel abroad for the treatment of his son.' Q supplies the subject and verb ('he decided'); 'decide' is followed by a to-infinitive, so P ('to travel abroad') comes next. R gives the purpose ('for the treatment'), and S ('of his son') must stay right after 'treatment' because it says whose treatment. Option A (PSQR) puts 'of his son' straight after 'abroad', which makes no sense, and leaves 'for the treatment' hanging at the end. Option B (RPQS) starts with the purpose phrase and separates 'treatment' from 'of his son'. Option D (PRSQ), 'To travel abroad for the treatment of his son he decided', is an inverted, poetic order that is not used in a plain statement. Rule to remember: verbs such as decide, plan, hope, agree, refuse, promise and want take 'to + verb' (He refused to sign; She agreed to help), while verbs such as enjoy, avoid and finish take the -ing form. Another example of the same pattern: 'She planned to move to Bhubaneswar for the education of her daughter.'

Q 81 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) a new laptop
(Q) for his school
(R) the teacher bought
(S) yesterday
(1) RSQP
(2) RPQS
(3) PSRQ
(4) QPRS
= 2
~ RPQS gives 'The teacher bought a new laptop for his school yesterday.' R contains the subject and verb ('the teacher bought'), P is the direct object ('a new laptop'), Q says for whom or for what purpose ('for his school'), and S, the time adverb ('yesterday'), comes at the end. A basic rule of English word order is that nothing should come between a verb and its direct object. Option A (RSQP) breaks this rule: 'bought yesterday for his school a new laptop' pushes the object to the very end. Option C (PSRQ) puts the object and the time word before the subject, and Option D (QPRS) begins with the purpose phrase followed by the object, giving an inverted order. The normal sequence is Subject + Verb + Object + Place/Purpose + Time. 'Yesterday' may also come first for emphasis ('Yesterday, the teacher bought…'), but that arrangement is not among the options. More examples: 'She posted the letter at the office this morning'; 'They held a meeting in the hall on Monday.' Tip: in sentence-rearrangement questions, join the verb and its object first, then add the other phrases around them.

Q 82 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) in her writing
(Q) significant improvements
(R) over the years
(S) she has made
(1) SQPR
(2) QSPR
(3) SRPQ
(4) RQSP
= 1
~ SQPR gives 'She has made significant improvements in her writing over the years.' S is the subject and verb ('she has made'), Q is the object ('significant improvements'), P tells us in what area ('in her writing'), and R, the time phrase ('over the years'), closes the sentence. Note two points. First, the noun 'improvement' takes 'in' when we name the area (improvement in health, improvement in writing). Second, 'over the years' describes a period running up to now, which is why the present perfect 'has made' is used; the same is true of 'since 2020', 'for five years' and 'so far'. Option B (QSPR) fronts the object ('Significant improvements she has made…'), a style used only for special emphasis, not in a plain statement. Option C (SRPQ) puts two phrases between the verb and its object. Option D (RQSP) also scrambles the order, placing the object before the subject. Another example of the same pattern: 'Odisha has made great progress in disaster management over the years.' Tip: a time phrase like 'over the years' or 'yesterday' usually goes at the end of the sentence.

Q 83 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) has been
(Q) reading romantic novels
(R) and going to sleep
(S) her habit
(1) SPRQ
(2) RSPQ
(3) QRPS
(4) PRSQ
= 3
~ QRPS gives 'Reading romantic novels and going to sleep has been her habit.' Here the subject is a gerund phrase: Q ('reading romantic novels') and R ('and going to sleep') join to name her routine, P ('has been') is the verb, and S ('her habit') is the complement. Gerunds (verb + -ing used as nouns) can be the subject of a sentence, as in 'Swimming is good exercise'. Because the two actions together form one habit, the singular 'has been' fits. Option A (SPRQ) gives 'Her habit has been and going to sleep reading romantic novels', which misplaces 'and' and splits the two gerunds. Option B (RSPQ) starts the sentence with 'and', and Option D (PRSQ) starts with the verb 'has been', so neither can open a statement. A natural alternative, 'Her habit has been reading romantic novels and going to sleep' (SPQR), is not offered, so QRPS is the only workable arrangement. Tip: a part beginning with 'and' must follow the item it joins. Compare: 'Walking and cycling are good exercises' (two activities, plural verb) with 'Bread and butter is his breakfast' (one idea, singular verb).

Q 84 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the four parts marked P, Q, R, S to make a correct sentence by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) my sister
(Q) likes to bake
(R) on weekends
(S) cookies
(1) PQSR
(2) PRQS
(3) PRSQ
(4) RPSQ
= 1
~ PQSR gives 'My sister likes to bake cookies on weekends.' P is the subject ('my sister'), Q the verb ('likes to bake'), S the object of 'bake' ('cookies'), and R the time phrase ('on weekends'). The object must follow its verb directly: we 'bake cookies', so nothing should separate the two. Option B (PRQS), 'My sister on weekends likes to bake cookies', wedges the time phrase between the subject and the verb, which is awkward without commas and is not the standard order. Options C (PRSQ) and D (RPSQ) both place 'cookies' before 'likes to bake', giving a verb-last order that English does not allow. Two extra points are worth remembering. 'Like' can take either 'to + verb' or the -ing form, so 'likes to bake' and 'likes baking' are both correct. 'On weekends' is the American form, while British English says 'at weekends'; both are accepted in exams. The safe order is Subject + Verb + Object + Place + Time, as in 'He plays cricket in the park on Sundays' and 'We visit Puri every December.'

Q 85 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the sentences marked P, Q, R, S to make a coherent paragraph by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) The strategy was promising and showed significant results.
(Q) So, the management decided to expand the trial to more regions.
(R) A new marketing strategy was piloted in select regions.
(S) Customer feedback during the pilot was overwhelmingly positive.
(1) QRPS
(2) PSQR
(3) RPSQ
(4) SRQP
= 3
~ RPSQ gives a logical paragraph. R opens it by introducing the topic with the indefinite article: 'A new marketing strategy was piloted in select regions.' P then refers back with the definite article ('The strategy was promising and showed significant results'), S adds supporting evidence from the same pilot ('Customer feedback during the pilot was overwhelmingly positive'), and Q gives the result, signalled by 'So': the management decided to expand the trial to more regions. Option A (QRPS) begins with 'So', which can never open a paragraph because it needs a cause before it. Option B (PSQR) opens with 'The strategy', but we do not yet know which strategy. Option D (SRQP) begins with 'during the pilot' before any pilot has been mentioned, and it ends with results after the decision. Method for paragraph jumbles: find the sentence that can stand alone (usually one with 'a/an' and no pronoun or connector), then follow the pointers such as 'the', 'this', 'it', 'also', 'so' and 'however', and place the conclusion or decision last. Checking only the first and last sentences often eliminates two options quickly.

Q 86 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the sentences marked P, Q, R, S to make a coherent paragraph by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) This influx strained local infrastructure and resources.
(Q) The scenic valley became a major tourist destination.
(R) Consequently, authorities implemented visitor quotas.
(S) Its popularity surged after a viral social media campaign.
(1) PRQS
(2) SQRP
(3) RPSQ
(4) QSPR
= 4
~ QSPR gives the paragraph: the scenic valley became a major tourist destination (Q); its popularity surged after a viral social media campaign (S); this influx strained local infrastructure and resources (P); consequently, authorities implemented visitor quotas (R). Q is the only sentence that can start the paragraph, because it names the valley. S uses the possessive 'Its', which needs a noun before it (the valley). P begins with 'This influx', a summary phrase that refers to the flood of tourists just described. R starts with 'Consequently', so it must report the final result of that strain. Option A (PRQS) opens with 'This influx', Option B (SQRP) with 'Its popularity', and Option C (RPSQ) with 'Consequently'; none of these can begin a paragraph because each points back to something not yet said. Chain to remember: situation → cause → effect → response. Words like 'this', 'its', 'such', 'consequently', 'therefore' and 'as a result' are links, never opening lines. Another chain of the same kind: 'The festival drew huge crowds. This rush caused traffic jams. Therefore, the police diverted vehicles.' Vocabulary: influx = a large number arriving.

Q 87 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the sentences marked P, Q, R, S to make a coherent paragraph by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) This lack of evidence led to the case being dismissed.
(Q) The investigation uncovered several inconsistencies in the testimony.
(R) Some people raised serious allegations against the official.
(S) Forensic analysis also failed to corroborate the claims.
(1) RQSP
(2) QRPS
(3) PSRQ
(4) SRQP
= 1
~ RQSP gives the logical order. R introduces the subject: some people raised serious allegations against the official. Q reports the first finding: the investigation uncovered several inconsistencies in the testimony. S adds a second weakness with the word 'also': forensic analysis also failed to corroborate (confirm) the claims. P closes with the outcome: 'This lack of evidence led to the case being dismissed.' Option B (QRPS) mentions 'The investigation' before any allegation, and it places the dismissal before the forensic point, so the 'also' of S would come after the case is already over. Option C (PSRQ) opens with 'This lack of evidence', which needs something before it to refer to. Option D (SRQP) opens with S, but 'also' and 'the claims' both point back to earlier sentences. Tips: 'also' marks an added point, so it cannot appear in the first sentence; summary words such as 'this lack', 'this decision' and 'such steps' usually come near the end. Vocabulary: corroborate = confirm with evidence; testimony = a witness's statement; dismissed (of a case) = rejected by the court.

Q 88 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the sentences marked P, Q, R, S to make a coherent paragraph by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) This decentralization enhances security and transparency.
(Q) Blockchain technology operates on a distributed ledger system.
(R) Each participant in the network holds a copy of the ledger.
(S) Transactions are recorded simultaneously across multiple nodes.
(1) SPQR
(2) RSQP
(3) QRSP
(4) PQRS
= 3
~ QRSP moves from definition to working to benefit. Q introduces the topic: blockchain technology operates on a distributed ledger system. R explains the first feature: each participant in the network holds a copy of the ledger ('the ledger' points back to Q). S adds how entries are made: transactions are recorded simultaneously across multiple nodes. P sums up with the benefit: 'This decentralization enhances security and transparency', where 'This decentralization' refers to the shared copies and multiple nodes described in R and S. Option A (SPQR) starts with transactions before blockchain has been introduced. Option B (RSQP) begins with 'the network' and 'the ledger', which are not yet known to the reader. Option D (PQRS) begins with 'This decentralization', which has nothing to refer to. General-to-specific is the commonest pattern in such questions: topic → explanation → result. Background useful for general studies: a blockchain is a shared, tamper-resistant record that no single authority controls; it was first used for the cryptocurrency Bitcoin (launched in 2009) and is now tried in areas such as land records, supply chains and digital certificates.

Q 89 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the sentences marked P, Q, R, S to make a coherent paragraph by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) However, the new policy is designed to benefit everyone in the long run.
(Q) It is aimed to increase efficiency in the office.
(R) Recently, the company announced a new incentive policy.
(S) Initially, the clerks are likely to face a few challenges.
(1) RPQS
(2) RQSP
(3) RPSQ
(4) SRPQ
= 2
~ RQSP gives: 'Recently, the company announced a new incentive policy (R). It is aimed to increase efficiency in the office (Q). Initially, the clerks are likely to face a few challenges (S). However, the new policy is designed to benefit everyone in the long run (P).' R is the natural opener: 'Recently' sets the time and 'a new incentive policy' introduces the topic. Q follows because its pronoun 'It' needs the policy as its antecedent. S raises a short-term problem with 'Initially', and P answers it with 'However … in the long run', so 'initially' and 'in the long run' form a matching pair. Options A (RPQS) and C (RPSQ) put 'However' straight after the announcement, when there is nothing yet to contrast, and they push the policy's aim or its early problems after the conclusion. Option D (SRPQ) opens with the clerks' challenges before the policy has been mentioned. Tip: 'however', 'but' and 'yet' introduce a contrast, so the point being contrasted must come just before them. Grammar note: the better phrasing is 'It is aimed at increasing efficiency' or 'It aims to increase efficiency'.

Q 90 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Rearrange the sentences marked P, Q, R, S to make a coherent paragraph by selecting the best option from the choices given below each.
(P) However, critics argue it stifles artistic freedom and innovation.
(Q) The film industry relies heavily on established franchises and sequels.
(R) This strategy minimizes financial risk for studios.
(S) Audiences often show strong loyalty to familiar characters and stories.
(1) PRQS
(2) SQPR
(3) RPSQ
(4) QSRP
= 4
~ QSRP builds the paragraph as trend → reason → advantage → criticism. Q states the trend: the film industry relies heavily on established franchises and sequels. S gives the reason: audiences often show strong loyalty to familiar characters and stories. R names the advantage, and its phrase 'This strategy' refers back to the reliance on franchises: it minimizes financial risk for studios. P, opening with 'However', presents the opposite view: critics argue that it stifles (suppresses) artistic freedom and innovation. Options A (PRQS) and C (RPSQ) begin with 'However' and 'This strategy', both of which need an earlier sentence. Option B (SQPR) opens with the audience's loyalty before the trend it explains, and it ends with the advantage after the criticism, so 'This strategy' sits far from the sentence it refers to. A reliable pattern in argument paragraphs is: present the practice, explain and support it, then introduce the counter-view with 'However', 'On the other hand' or 'Critics argue'. Vocabulary: franchise = a series of films built around the same characters; sequel = a later film that continues a story; stifle = to hold back or smother.

Q 91 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Read the passage given below carefully and answer the questions 91 to 95 by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
Globalization has reshaped education by enabling access to a vast array of resources and facilitating communication among students worldwide. Students can now interact with peers and educators across the globe, learning from diverse perspectives. This connection fosters a greater understanding of global issues and cultural differences. Additionally, globalization promotes the adoption of international curriculums, such as the International Baccalaureate (IB), which are designed to cultivate skills needed in a global workforce. However, this shift can also lead to a neglect of local educational values and content, potentially diluting cultural identities. The increasing reliance on technology further complicates this landscape, as students must navigate online learning environments while developing critical thinking skills. Overall, while globalization enhances educational opportunities and cross-cultural exchanges, it also poses challenges that stakeholders must address to preserve local cultures and maintain educational integrity.
Globalization affected student interactions in education by ______.
(1) limiting communication
(2) enabling access to resources
(3) creating barriers to understanding
(4) discouraging cultural exchanges
= 2
~ The very first sentence of the passage gives the answer: globalization 'has reshaped education by enabling access to a vast array of resources and facilitating communication among students worldwide.' So 'enabling access to resources' is the phrase taken directly from the text. The next sentences add that students now interact with peers and teachers across the globe and learn from diverse perspectives, which supports the same point. The other choices say the opposite of the passage. Option A, limiting communication, contradicts 'facilitating communication'. Option C, creating barriers to understanding, contradicts 'fosters a greater understanding of global issues and cultural differences'. Option D, discouraging cultural exchanges, contradicts the final sentence, which says globalization enhances 'cross-cultural exchanges'. Technique for fact-based questions: pick out the keywords in the question (here 'globalization' and 'education'), scan the passage for the sentence that contains them, and choose the option that matches its meaning. Options that reverse the passage, using words like limiting, barriers or discouraging, are the commonest traps in reading comprehension.

Q 92 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
(Passage as in Q91.)
What international curriculum is mentioned as being promoted by globalization?
(1) National Core Curriculum
(2) Advanced Placement
(3) International Baccalaureate
(4) Traditional Local Curriculum
= 3
~ The passage names only one international curriculum: globalization 'promotes the adoption of international curriculums, such as the International Baccalaureate (IB), which are designed to cultivate skills needed in a global workforce.' The other choices are not mentioned. A national core curriculum is the opposite of an international one. Advanced Placement (AP) is a real programme of college-level courses run by the College Board in the United States, but the passage never refers to it. A traditional local curriculum is what the passage says may be neglected, not promoted. Background: the IB was set up in Geneva, Switzerland, in 1968 and offers programmes for school students, including the two-year Diploma Programme. In India, IB schools exist alongside boards such as CBSE, CISCE and the state boards, which in Odisha are the Board of Secondary Education (BSE) and the Council of Higher Secondary Education (CHSE). Exam tip: in 'which is mentioned' questions, answer only from the text. An option may be true in the real world, as AP is, and still be wrong because the passage does not say it.

Q 93 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
(Passage as in Q91.)
What challenge does globalization pose to local education according to the passage?
(1) Enhanced technological resources
(2) An emphasis on standardized tests
(3) Increased cultural understanding
(4) Neglect of local educational values
= 4
~ The passage turns from benefits to problems with the word 'However': 'this shift can also lead to a neglect of local educational values and content, potentially diluting cultural identities.' That neglect is the challenge globalization poses to local education, so option D is correct. Option A, enhanced technological resources, is not described as a challenge; the passage says only that the 'reliance on technology further complicates this landscape', which is a different point. Option B, an emphasis on standardized tests, is never mentioned. Option C, increased cultural understanding, is presented as a benefit ('This connection fosters a greater understanding of global issues and cultural differences'), the opposite of a challenge. Technique: when a question asks about a drawback, limitation or challenge, look first for contrast words such as however, but, yet or on the other hand, because the writer usually shifts to the negative side there. Vocabulary from the sentence: neglect = lack of proper care or attention; dilute = weaken, as if by mixing with water; cultural identity = the sense of belonging that comes from a group's language, customs and values.

Q 94 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
(Passage as in Q91.)
What critical skill must students develop while navigating online learning environments?
(1) Critical thinking skills
(2) Memorization
(3) Basic computer knowledge
(4) Time management
= 1
~ The passage says, 'The increasing reliance on technology further complicates this landscape, as students must navigate online learning environments while developing critical thinking skills.' So the skill students must build while working online is critical thinking: the ability to analyse information, question sources and form reasoned judgements. Notice how closely the question copies the text: 'navigate online learning environments' appears in both, which tells you exactly where to look. Option B, memorization, is not mentioned and is almost the opposite of critical thinking. Option C, basic computer knowledge, sounds sensible for online learning, but the passage does not name it. Option D, time management, is also a useful skill that the passage never mentions. The trap here is choosing an answer from general knowledge rather than from the passage. In reading comprehension, an option must be supported by the text, however reasonable it sounds. Critical thinking matters especially online because students meet a flood of information, including misleading content, and must judge what is reliable before using it.

Q 95 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
(Passage as in Q91.)
The overall effect does the passage suggest globalization has on education is that it ______.
(1) diminishes local identities
(2) restricts educational opportunities
(3) enhances educational opportunities and cultural exchanges
(4) discourages international collaboration
= 3
~ The last sentence of the passage gives its overall verdict: 'Overall, while globalization enhances educational opportunities and cross-cultural exchanges, it also poses challenges that stakeholders must address.' The word 'Overall' signals the summary, and its main idea is exactly option C: globalization enhances educational opportunities and cultural exchanges. Option A, diminishes local identities, picks up only one challenge from the middle of the passage ('potentially diluting cultural identities'); it is a part, not the overall effect, and the passage even softens it with 'potentially'. Option B, restricts educational opportunities, contradicts the passage, which says globalization enables access to a vast array of resources. Option D, discourages international collaboration, also contradicts it, since students now interact with peers and educators across the globe. Technique: for questions on the main idea, overall effect or tone, read the first and last sentences first, because writers often state their point at the start and sum it up at the end with words like overall, in short, thus or to conclude. Then reject any option that is too narrow (a single detail) or that reverses the author's view.

Q 96 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
Directions : Read the passage given below carefully and answer the questions 96 to 100 by selecting the most appropriate option from the choices given below each.
The State of Odisha has emerged as a pivotal force in Indian sports, primarily through its transformative investments in hockey. Hosting the 2018 Men's Hockey World Cup in Bhubaneswar and Rourkela-the latter featuring the world's largest hockey stadium-signalled its infrastructural ambition. The state government's corporate patronage, notably via PSUs like Odisha Mining Corporation, funds elite athlete development, including Olympic medallists in hockey and athletics. Beyond infrastructure, Odisha pioneered grassroots talent identification in tribal regions, focusing on hockey, rugby, and weightlifting. Its “holistic development” model integrates sports science, education, and international coaching partnerships. However, critics note disproportionate hockey funding overshadows emerging disciplines like gymnastics. Odisha's sustained commitment positions it as an indispensable catalyst for India's sporting ascendancy.
What sporting discipline is emphasized as receiving more investment compared to emerging fields like gymnastics?
(1) Athletics
(2) Hockey
(3) Rugby
(4) Weightlifting
= 2
~ Hockey is the answer. The passage says Odisha became a force in Indian sports 'primarily through its transformative investments in hockey', and later notes the criticism that 'disproportionate hockey funding overshadows emerging disciplines like gymnastics'. 'Disproportionate' means too large compared with others, so hockey is the sport that receives more money than gymnastics. Athletics appears only in the line about Olympic medallists, while rugby and weightlifting appear only in the list of sports for tribal talent identification; none of them is said to be over-funded. Background that often appears in Odisha exams: the state has sponsored the Indian men's and women's hockey teams since 2018; the deal was renewed in 2023 until 2033 and, in June 2024, extended until 2036. Kalinga Stadium in Bhubaneswar hosted the 2018 Men's Hockey World Cup, and Bhubaneswar and Rourkela co-hosted the 2023 edition. Tip: when a question repeats a phrase from the passage ('emerging fields like gymnastics'), go straight to that sentence; the answer is usually in the same line.

Q 97 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
(Passage as in Q96.)
How does the passage characterize the state government's role in funding elite athletes?
(1) Primarily reliant on federal grants
(2) Centred on private-sector donations
(3) Focused on crowdfunding initiatives
(4) Driven by corporate patronage via PSUs
= 4
~ The passage states: 'The state government's corporate patronage, notably via PSUs like Odisha Mining Corporation, funds elite athlete development, including Olympic medallists in hockey and athletics.' So the funding is described as corporate patronage through public sector undertakings (PSUs), that is, government-owned companies, which matches option D. Option A, federal grants, would mean money from the central government; the passage does not mention it. Option B, private-sector donations, is wrong because a PSU is a public company, not a private one. Option C, crowdfunding (raising small amounts from many members of the public, usually online), is not mentioned at all. Vocabulary: patronage = support or sponsorship given by a patron; elite = the best, top level. Background: the Odisha Mining Corporation (OMC) is a state-owned mining company, and Odisha is India's leading producer of iron ore, chromite and bauxite, which is why its mining PSUs have money to spend. Trap to avoid: option B looks tempting because 'corporate' suggests private business, but read the full phrase 'via PSUs', which points to state-owned companies.

Q 98 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
(Passage as in Q96.)
Which aspect of Odisha's sports model is explicitly described as “holistic”?
(1) Integration of sports science and education
(2) Talent identification in tribal regions
(3) Construction of mega-stadiums
(4) Hosting international tournaments
= 1
~ The passage says, 'Its "holistic development" model integrates sports science, education, and international coaching partnerships.' The only aspect tied to the word 'holistic' is therefore the integration of sports science and education (together with coaching partnerships), which is option A. 'Holistic' means dealing with the whole rather than with separate parts: here the athlete's training, scientific support and schooling are planned together. Option B, talent identification in tribal regions, is mentioned in the previous sentence as a grassroots effort, not as the holistic model. Option C, construction of mega-stadiums, refers to infrastructure, and Option D, hosting international tournaments, refers to the 2018 World Cup; both appear earlier and are not called holistic. Technique: when a question quotes a word in inverted commas ('holistic'), locate that exact word in the passage and read the sentence around it. Options taken from other sentences of the same passage are typical distractors: they are true statements, but they do not answer the question asked. Related word: 'integrate' means to combine separate parts into one system.

Q 99 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
(Passage as in Q96.)
What unique demographic focus is cited in Odisha's grassroots sports strategy?
(1) Urban youth in metropolitan areas
(2) Coastal fishing communities
(3) Tribal regions
(4) High-income private schools
= 3
~ The passage says, 'Odisha pioneered grassroots talent identification in tribal regions, focusing on hockey, rugby, and weightlifting.' The demographic (population group) targeted is therefore people in tribal regions, option C. 'Grassroots' means at the most basic, local level, so the state looks for talent among young people in villages rather than only among established athletes. Option A, urban youth in metropolitan areas, Option B, coastal fishing communities, and Option D, high-income private schools, are not mentioned anywhere in the passage. Background that suits an Odisha exam: Sundargarh district, a largely tribal area in north-western Odisha, is known as the cradle of Indian hockey; Olympians such as Dilip Tirkey and Amit Rohidas come from this district, and the Birsa Munda International Hockey Stadium at Rourkela, opened in January 2023, seats about 20,000 spectators. Tip: words in the question such as 'unique' or 'specific' usually point to one precise phrase in the text, here 'tribal regions'. Do not pick an option just because it sounds like a sensible policy.

Q 100 | eng | Comprehension & Usage
(Passage as in Q96.)
The hosting of the 2018 Men's Hockey World Cup specifically highlighted Odisha's advancement in which area?
(1) Sports infrastructure
(2) Corporate sponsorship models
(3) Grassroots talent identification
(4) International coaching partnerships
= 1
~ The passage says that hosting the 2018 Men's Hockey World Cup, together with 'the world's largest hockey stadium' at Rourkela, 'signalled its infrastructural ambition'. The very next sentence begins 'Beyond infrastructure…', which confirms that the World Cup example was about sports infrastructure, option A. Option B, corporate sponsorship models, refers to the PSU funding sentence; Option C, grassroots talent identification, belongs to the tribal-regions sentence; and Option D, international coaching partnerships, is part of the 'holistic development' model. Each is true of Odisha, but none is linked to the World Cup in the passage. A factual note: the 2018 Men's Hockey World Cup was played only at Kalinga Stadium, Bhubaneswar. Rourkela's Birsa Munda International Hockey Stadium (about 20,000 seats, opened in January 2023) co-hosted the 2023 World Cup with Bhubaneswar. The passage merges the two editions, but in comprehension you answer from the text, not from outside facts. Tip: phrases such as 'Beyond X', 'Apart from X' and 'In addition to X' tell you that the earlier sentences were about X.

Q 101 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
ଠିକ୍ ଶବ୍ଦ (ଉତ୍ତର) ଚିହ୍ନଟ କର
(1) ପରିଶଦ
(2) ପରିସଦ
(3) ପରିଷଦ
(4) ପରୀଷଦ
= 3
~ ପରିଷଦ (council, assembly) is the correct spelling. It is a Sanskrit tatsama word made of the prefix ପରି and the root ସଦ୍ ('to sit'): literally 'those who sit around', hence a gathering. Among the Odia ଉଷ୍ମ ବର୍ଣ୍ଣ there are three s-letters: palatal ଶ (ତାଲବ୍ୟ), retroflex ଷ (ମୂର୍ଦ୍ଧନ୍ୟ) and dental ସ (ଦନ୍ତ୍ୟ). By the ṣatva rule (ଷତ୍ୱ ବିଧାନ), the dental ସ of certain roots becomes ଷ after a prefix ending in ଇ or ଉ: ଅଭି + ସେକ = ଅଭିଷେକ, ନି + ସେଧ = ନିଷେଧ, ପ୍ରତି + ସ୍ଥା = ପ୍ରତିଷ୍ଠା. In the same way ପରି + ସଦ = ପରିଷଦ. Option A (ପରିଶଦ) uses the palatal ଶ, option B (ପରିସଦ) keeps the unchanged dental ସ, and option D (ପରୀଷଦ) wrongly lengthens the prefix ପରି, which has a short ି (ପରିବାର, ପରିବେଶ, ପରିଚୟ); the ୀ of ପରୀକ୍ଷା comes only from sandhi (ପରି + ଈକ୍ଷା). Trap: the rule does not apply to every word, since ପରିସର and ପରିସ୍ଥିତି keep the dental ସ, so learn ପରିଷଦ as a fixed form. Usage: ମନ୍ତ୍ରିପରିଷଦ (Council of Ministers), ବିଧାନ ପରିଷଦ (Legislative Council) and ଜିଲ୍ଲା ପରିଷଦ, the top tier of Odisha's three-tier Panchayati Raj.

Q 102 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
ଠିକ୍ ଶବ୍ଦ (ଉତ୍ତର) ଚିହ୍ନଟ କର
(1) ଅବଷର୍ଣ
(2) ଅବସନ
(3) ଅବଶନ୍ନ
(4) ଅବସନ୍ନ
= 4
~ ଅବସନ୍ନ (tired, exhausted, low in spirit) is the correct spelling. It is a Sanskrit tatsama word: the prefix ଅବ + the root ସଦ୍ ('to sink, to sit down') + the past-participle suffix -ତ. When ସଦ୍ takes this suffix, both the d and the t turn into n, giving the double conjunct ନ୍ନ: ସଦ୍ + ତ = ସନ୍ନ. The same root gives ପ୍ରସନ୍ନ (pleased), ଆସନ୍ନ (near, about to come) and the noun ଅବସାଦ (fatigue, depression). Because the prefix ଅବ ends in 'a', the dental ସ stays unchanged. Compare ବିଷଣ୍ଣ (sad), from ବି + ସଦ୍: after the 'i' of ବି, ସ becomes ଷ and ନ becomes ଣ. Option A (ଅବଷର୍ଣ) has the wrong letters altogether (ଷ and ର୍ଣ). Option B (ଅବସନ) drops the conjunct and writes a single ନ. Option C (ଅବଶନ୍ନ) uses the palatal ଶ instead of the dental ସ. Usage: 'ଦିନସାରା ପରିଶ୍ରମ ପରେ ଶରୀର ଅବସନ୍ନ ହୋଇପଡ଼ିଲା' (after a whole day's work the body was exhausted). Memory tip: the ସଦ୍ family ends in -ସନ୍ନ: ଅବସନ୍ନ, ପ୍ରସନ୍ନ, ଆସନ୍ନ.

Q 103 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
ଠିକ୍ ଶବ୍ଦ (ଉତ୍ତର) ଚିହ୍ନଟ କର
(1) ଶଶିଭୂଷଣ
(2) ଶଶୀଭୂଷଣ
(3) ସଶିଭୂଷଣ
(4) ଶଶିଭୁଷଣ
= 1
~ ଶଶିଭୂଷଣ ('one whose ornament is the moon', a name of Lord Shiva) is the correct spelling. It is a compound of ଶଶୀ (the moon, literally 'the one marked with a hare') and ଭୂଷଣ (ornament). Rule: Sanskrit words ending in -ଇନ୍ (ଶଶିନ୍, ମନ୍ତ୍ରିନ୍, ପ୍ରାଣିନ୍) are written with a long ୀ when they stand alone (ଶଶୀ, ମନ୍ତ୍ରୀ, ପ୍ରାଣୀ), but they take the short stem form with ି as the first part of a compound: ଶଶିକଳା, ମନ୍ତ୍ରିମଣ୍ଡଳ, ପ୍ରାଣିବିଜ୍ଞାନ. Hence ଶଶି + ଭୂଷଣ = ଶଶିଭୂଷଣ. Both sibilants are the palatal ଶ, and ଭୂଷଣ has a long ୂ and a retroflex ଣ, because ନ turns into ଣ after ଷ (ṇatva rule, as in ଭୀଷଣ and ବର୍ଷଣ). Option B (ଶଶୀଭୂଷଣ) keeps the long ୀ inside the compound, option C (ସଶିଭୂଷଣ) begins with the dental ସ, and option D (ଶଶିଭୁଷଣ) shortens the ୂ of ଭୂଷଣ. Similar names of Shiva: ଶଶିଶେଖର, ଚନ୍ଦ୍ରଶେଖର, ଚନ୍ଦ୍ରଚୂଡ଼. Memory tip: 'alone long, in a compound short', so ଶଶୀ but ଶଶିମୁଖୀ, ମନ୍ତ୍ରୀ but ମନ୍ତ୍ରିପରିଷଦ.

Q 104 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
ଠିକ୍ ଶବ୍ଦ (ଉତ୍ତର) ଚିହ୍ନଟ କର
(1) ନୀରୂପମା
(2) ନିରୂପମା
(3) ନିରୁପମା
(4) ନୀରୁପମା
= 3
~ ନିରୁପମା ('matchless, beyond comparison'; also a popular girls' name) is the correct spelling. It comes from ନିଃ ('without') + ଉପମା ('comparison'), so it means 'one for whom there is no comparison'. By visarga sandhi, when the visarga of ନିଃ or ଦୁଃ is followed by a vowel or a voiced consonant, it becomes ର: ନିଃ + ଆଶା = ନିରାଶା, ନିଃ + ଆକାର = ନିରାକାର, ଦୁଃ + ଆଚାର = ଦୁରାଚାର, ନିଃ + ଉତ୍ତର = ନିରୁତ୍ତର. The vowels themselves do not change: ନି keeps its short ି and the ଉ of ଉପମା stays short, giving ନିରୁପମା. Option A (ନୀରୂପମା) lengthens both vowels. Option B (ନିରୂପମା) wrongly uses the long ୂ, probably by confusion with ନିରୂପଣ ('determining', a different word from ନି + ରୂପ). Option D (ନୀରୁପମା) lengthens ନି, probably by confusion with ନୀର ('water'). Contrast: ନିଃ + ଈହା = ନିରୀହ (harmless) has a long vowel only because ଈହା itself begins with ଈ. Rule of thumb: in ନିଃ/ଦୁଃ sandhi, copy the vowel of the second word exactly as it is.

Q 105 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
ଠିକ୍ ଶବ୍ଦ (ଉତ୍ତର) ଚିହ୍ନଟ କର
(1) ଭଗିନୀ
(2) ଭଗୀନୀ
(3) ଭଗିନି
(4) ଭଗୀନି
= 1
~ ଭଗିନୀ (sister) is the correct spelling. It is a Sanskrit tatsama word, and its everyday Odia (tadbhava) form is ଭଉଣୀ. Words built on an -ଇନ୍ base form their feminine by adding the long ending ଈ, so the middle syllable keeps a short ି and the last syllable takes a long ୀ, giving ଗି + ନୀ. The same pattern appears in ଯୋଗିନୀ, କାମିନୀ, ନନ୍ଦିନୀ, ମାଳିନୀ, ତପସ୍ୱିନୀ and ଯଶସ୍ୱିନୀ, and, with ଣ after ର or ଋ, in ଗୃହିଣୀ and ତରଙ୍ଗିଣୀ. Option B (ଭଗୀନୀ) wrongly lengthens the middle ି; option C (ଭଗିନି) shortens the feminine ending; option D (ଭଗୀନି) reverses both, putting the long vowel in the middle and the short one at the end. Usage: ଭଗିନୀ ନିବେଦିତା (Sister Nivedita, the disciple of Swami Vivekananda), and the formal pair ଭ୍ରାତା-ଭଗିନୀ (brothers and sisters); in everyday speech a public address begins 'ପ୍ରିୟ ଭାଇ ଓ ଭଉଣୀମାନେ'. Memory tip: in feminine words ending in -ଇନୀ, think 'short first, long last' (ି … ୀ), exactly as in ନନ୍ଦିନୀ.

Q 106 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
ଠିକ୍ ଶବ୍ଦ (ଉତ୍ତର) ଚିହ୍ନଟ କର
(1) ତ୍ରୁଟୀ
(2) ତ୍ରୁଟି
(3) ତୃଟୀ
(4) ତୃଟି
= 2
~ ତ୍ରୁଟି (fault, defect, shortcoming) is the correct spelling. It is the Sanskrit tatsama word truṭi, so it begins with the conjunct ତ୍ର (ତ + ର-ଫଳା) carrying the u-sign ୁ, and ends with a short ି. The main trap is the vowel sign ୃ (ଋ-କାର). In Odia, ୃ is pronounced 'ru', so ତ୍ରୁ and ତୃ sound almost the same, but they are written differently: ୃ appears in words such as ତୃଣ (grass), ତୃଷା (thirst), ତୃପ୍ତି, କୃଷି and ବୃକ୍ଷ, while ତ୍ରୁ appears in ତ୍ରୁଟି and ଶତ୍ରୁ (enemy). Option A (ତ୍ରୁଟୀ) has the right beginning but a long final ୀ. Option C (ତୃଟୀ) is wrong twice: ୃ and a long ending. Option D (ତୃଟି) has the right ending but the wrong ୃ. Related forms: ତ୍ରୁଟିପୂର୍ଣ୍ଣ (faulty), ତ୍ରୁଟିହୀନ (flawless) and the common office phrase ତ୍ରୁଟି-ବିଚ୍ୟୁତି (lapses and errors), a useful expression for an Assistant Section Officer drafting notes and letters. Memory tip: ତ୍ରୁଟି and ଶତ୍ରୁ share the same ତ୍ରୁ, so think 'a fault is the enemy of good work'.

Q 107 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
ଠିକ୍ ଶବ୍ଦ (ଉତ୍ତର) ଚିହ୍ନଟ କର
(1) ଗାରିମା
(2) ଗରିମା
(3) ଗରୀମା
(4) ଗାରୀମା
= 2
~ ଗରିମା (greatness, dignity, glory) is the correct spelling. In Sanskrit, abstract nouns of quality are formed with the suffix -ଇମନ୍, which gives words ending in -ଇମା with a short ି; before this suffix ଗୁରୁ ('heavy, great') takes the form ଗର୍, so ଗୁରୁ + ଇମନ୍ = ଗରିମା. Its sister words are ମହିମା (from ମହତ୍), ଲଘିମା (from ଲଘୁ) and ଅଣିମା (from ଅଣୁ), which, together with ଗରିମା, are four of the eight yogic powers (ଅଷ୍ଟସିଦ୍ଧି). Option A (ଗାରିମା) adds a wrong ା to the first letter; option C (ଗରୀମା) lengthens the ି; option D (ଗାରୀମା) makes both mistakes. Trap to avoid: the comparative ଗରୀୟସୀ ('greater') does have a long ୀ, as in 'ଜନନୀ ଜନ୍ମଭୂମିଶ୍ଚ ସ୍ୱର୍ଗାଦପି ଗରୀୟସୀ' (mother and motherland are greater than heaven), while the superlative is ଗରିଷ୍ଠ, as in ସଂଖ୍ୟାଗରିଷ୍ଠ (majority). Usage: 'ଓଡ଼ିଆ ଭାଷାର ଗରିମା' (the glory of Odia, which became a classical language in 2014). Memory tip: the four siddhi words ending in -ଇମା all have a short ି: ଅଣିମା, ମହିମା, ଲଘିମା, ଗରିମା.

Q 108 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
ଠିକ୍ ଶବ୍ଦ (ଉତ୍ତର) ଚିହ୍ନଟ କର
(1) ଆନୁଷଙ୍ଗିକ
(2) ଆନୁସଙ୍ଗୀକ
(3) ଆନୁସଂଗିକ
(4) ଆନୂଷଙ୍ଗିକ
= 1
~ ଆନୁଷଙ୍ଗିକ (incidental, accompanying, related) is the correct spelling. It comes from ଅନୁଷଙ୍ଗ ('connection, attachment', from ଅନୁ + the root ସଞ୍ଜ୍, 'to cling') + the suffix -ଇକ. Three rules work together. First, the suffix -ଇକ lengthens the first vowel of the base (vṛddhi): ଅ → ଆ, just as ସମାଜ → ସାମାଜିକ, ଧର୍ମ → ଧାର୍ମିକ, ଦିନ → ଦୈନିକ. Second, after the ଉ of the prefix ଅନୁ the dental ସ becomes the retroflex ଷ (ṣatva rule, compare ଅନୁଷ୍ଠାନ). Third, the suffix -ଇକ always has a short ି, and the nasal before ଗ is the velar ଙ, written ଙ୍ଗ. Option B (ଆନୁସଙ୍ଗୀକ) keeps the dental ସ and lengthens the suffix; option C (ଆନୁସଂଗିକ) also keeps the dental ସ (the anusvara ଂ before ଗ is only a print variant of ଙ୍ଗ); option D (ଆନୂଷଙ୍ଗିକ) lengthens the ୁ of ନୁ. The word is common in office Odia: ଆନୁଷଙ୍ଗିକ ଖର୍ଚ୍ଚ (incidental expenses), ଆନୁଷଙ୍ଗିକ କାର୍ଯ୍ୟ (related work). Memory tip: -ଇକ adjectives always end in a short 'ିକ': ସାମାଜିକ, ଐତିହାସିକ, ଭୌଗୋଳିକ, ଆନୁଷଙ୍ଗିକ.

Q 109 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
ଠିକ୍ ଶବ୍ଦ (ଉତ୍ତର) ଚିହ୍ନଟ କର
(1) ମଧୂଳିକା
(2) ମଧୁଳିକା
(3) ମଧୂଳୀକା
(4) ମଧୁଳିକା
= 2,4
~ OPSC's key takes ମଧୁଳିକା as the correct spelling: a short ୁ on ଧ, as in ମଧୁ (honey), and a short ି on ଳ before the suffix -କା. Options B and D were printed identically as ମଧୁଳିକା, so the final key accepts both (the provisional key had only B). Odia usually writes the retroflex ଳ where Sanskrit has l between vowels (ବାଳିକା, କମଳ), and the suffix -ଇକା keeps a short ି, as in ବାଳିକା, ପୁସ୍ତିକା, ନାୟିକା and ଗୀତିକା. Option A (ମଧୂଳିକା) has a long ୂ on ଧ, and option C (ମଧୂଳୀକା) has both a long ୂ and a long ୀ; under the key both are wrong. Note for careful students: Sanskrit dictionaries record both madhulikā (for example, black mustard) and madhūlikā with a long ū (a kind of bee, a grain and some other plants), so the long-vowel form is not unknown; the exam, however, treats ମଧୁଳିକା, built directly on ମଧୁ, as the standard form. The same short ୁ appears in related words such as ମଧୁକର (bee), ମଧୁର (sweet) and ମଧୁମାସ (spring). (Official key; some sources differ.)

Q 110 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
ଠିକ୍ ଶବ୍ଦ (ଉତ୍ତର) ଚିହ୍ନଟ କର
(1) ତ୍ୱୀଷାଂପତୀ
(2) ତ୍ୱୀଷାଂପତି
(3) ତିଷାଂପତୀ
(4) ତ୍ୱିଷାଂପତି
= 4
~ ତ୍ୱିଷାଂପତି (the Sun, literally 'lord of the rays') is the correct spelling. It joins ତ୍ୱିଷାମ୍, the genitive plural of ତ୍ୱିଷ୍ ('light, ray', so 'of the rays'), with ପତି ('lord'). This is an aluk samāsa, a compound that keeps the case ending of its first word, like ବାଚସ୍ପତି ('lord of speech') and ଯୁଧିଷ୍ଠିର ('steady in battle'). Spelling points: the word starts with the conjunct ତ୍ୱ (ତ + ୱ-ଫଳା) with a short ି; then comes the retroflex ଷ with ା and an anusvara (ଷାଂ); and ପତି ends with a short ି, as it always does (ଭୂପତି, ସେନାପତି, ରାଷ୍ଟ୍ରପତି). Option A (ତ୍ୱୀଷାଂପତୀ) lengthens both i-sounds; option B (ତ୍ୱୀଷାଂପତି) lengthens the first; option C (ତିଷାଂପତୀ) drops the ୱ-ଫଳା and lengthens the ending. The Amarakosha lists ତ୍ୱିଷାଂପତି among the names of the Sun, along with ଅହର୍ପତି, ଦିବାକର, ଭାସ୍କର, ପ୍ରଭାକର and ମିହିର. Odisha link: the Sun Temple at Konark, a UNESCO World Heritage Site, is dedicated to this god. Memory tip: the word ପତି always ends in a short ି.

Q 111 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘ବ୍ୟର୍ଥ’ ର ବିପରୀତ :
(1) ସଫଳ
(2) ସାର୍ଥ
(3) ସମର୍ଥ
(4) ସ୍ୱାର୍ଥ
= 2
~ ସାର୍ଥ, usually written ସାର୍ଥକ ('meaningful, fruitful, worthwhile'), is the antonym of ବ୍ୟର୍ଥ ('futile, in vain'). Both are built on ଅର୍ଥ ('purpose, meaning'). ବ୍ୟର୍ଥ = ବି ('without') + ଅର୍ଥ, joined by yaṇ sandhi (ଇ + ଅ = ଯ, as in ଇତି + ଆଦି = ଇତ୍ୟାଦି and ଅତି + ଅନ୍ତ = ଅତ୍ୟନ୍ତ). ସାର୍ଥ = ସ ('with') + ଅର୍ଥ, joined by dīrgha sandhi (ଅ + ଅ = ଆ). So one word means 'without purpose' and the other 'with purpose', a perfect pair of opposites. The distractors each have their own partners: ସଫଳ (successful) ↔ ବିଫଳ, ସମର୍ଥ (capable) ↔ ଅସମର୍ଥ, and ସ୍ୱାର୍ଥ (self-interest) ↔ ପରାର୍ଥ (the good of others). Trap: in everyday speech 'ବ୍ୟର୍ଥ ହେଲା' can mean 'failed', which tempts you towards ସଫଳ, but the exam prefers the pair formed on the same base word. Usage: 'ତାଙ୍କ ପରିଶ୍ରମ ବ୍ୟର୍ଥ ହେଲା ନାହିଁ, ସାର୍ଥକ ହେଲା' (his effort was not wasted; it bore fruit). Memory tip: pick the option that keeps the root and flips the prefix (ବି ↔ ସ).

Q 112 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘ଭୀମ’ର ବିପରୀତ :
(1) କୋମଳ
(2) ଶାନ୍ତ
(3) କାନ୍ତ
(4) ଧୀର
= 3
~ କାନ୍ତ ('lovely, pleasing, charming') is the antonym of ଭୀମ ('terrible, fearsome', from the root ଭୀ, 'to fear', which also gives ଭୟ and ଭୀଷଣ). The pair is famous from Kalidasa's Raghuvamsha, where King Dilipa is said to have ଭୀମକାନ୍ତ qualities: he was both dreaded and loved, just as the ocean is feared for its sea-monsters yet sought for its gems. The other options have their own standard opposites: କୋମଳ (soft) ↔ କଠୋର; ଶାନ୍ତ (calm) ↔ ଅଶାନ୍ତ or ଉଗ୍ର; ଧୀର (patient, steady) ↔ ଅଧୀର or ଚଞ୍ଚଳ. Trap: ଶାନ୍ତ and କୋମଳ feel like natural opposites of 'fearsome', but the classical, listed pair is ଭୀମ–କାନ୍ତ. Note also that କାନ୍ତ as a noun means 'beloved, husband' (feminine କାନ୍ତା), as in ଶ୍ରୀକାନ୍ତ, 'beloved of Lakshmi'. Usage: 'ସମୁଦ୍ରର ଭୀମ ଓ କାନ୍ତ ଦୁଇ ରୂପ' (the sea's fearsome and lovely faces), which anyone who has seen the beach at Puri on a stormy day and on a calm one will understand. Memory tip: ଭୀମ scares, କାନ୍ତ charms.

Q 113 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘ମୁକୁଳା’ର ବିପରୀତ :
(1) ବଂଧନ
(2) ଶୃଙ୍ଖଳା
(3) ଶୃଙ୍ଖଳ
(4) ବଂଧା
= 4
~ ବନ୍ଧା (printed here as ବଂଧା), meaning 'tied, bound, fastened', is the antonym of ମୁକୁଳା ('loose, untied, free'). Both are everyday Odia adjectives: ମୁକୁଳା ବାଳ (loose hair) ↔ ବନ୍ଧା ବାଳ (tied hair); ମୁକୁଳା ଗାଈ (a cow let loose) ↔ ବନ୍ଧା ଗାଈ (a tethered cow). The related verbs are ମୁକୁଳିବା (to get free) and ବାନ୍ଧିବା (to tie). The key rule for antonym questions is that the answer must belong to the same part of speech as the given word. ମୁକୁଳା is an adjective, so its opposite must also be an adjective, and only ବନ୍ଧା qualifies. ବନ୍ଧନ (bond, bondage) and ଶୃଙ୍ଖଳ (chain) are nouns; they are the opposites of ମୁକ୍ତି or ସ୍ୱାଧୀନତା, not of ମୁକୁଳା. ଶୃଙ୍ଖଳା means order or discipline, and its own antonym is ବିଶୃଙ୍ଖଳା (disorder). Spelling note: OPSC prints the nasal as an anusvara (ବଂଧା, like ଗଂଗା in another question), but the standard form uses the conjunct ନ୍ଧ: ବନ୍ଧା, ବନ୍ଧନ. Memory tip: match adjective with adjective and noun with noun before you even think about meaning.

Q 114 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘ପ୍ରବର୍ତ୍ତନ’ର ବିପରୀତ :
(1) ନିବାରଣ
(2) ନିବୃତ୍ତି
(3) ନିବର୍ତ୍ତନ
(4) ନିର୍ବହଣ
= 3
~ ନିବର୍ତ୍ତନ ('turning back, withdrawal, stopping') is the antonym of ପ୍ରବର୍ତ୍ତନ ('setting in motion, introduction, launching'). Both come from the same root ବୃତ୍ ('to turn, to move') with opposite prefixes: ପ୍ର- ('forward') and ନି- ('back, down'). This same-root, opposite-prefix pattern gives several pairs: ପ୍ରବୃତ୍ତି (inclination to act) ↔ ନିବୃତ୍ତି (withdrawal), ପ୍ରବୃତ୍ତ ↔ ନିବୃତ୍ତ, and ପ୍ରବର୍ତ୍ତନ ↔ ନିବର୍ତ୍ତନ. Option B (ନିବୃତ୍ତି) is a trap: it is the opposite of ପ୍ରବୃତ୍ତି, not of ପ୍ରବର୍ତ୍ତନ, so the endings do not match. ନିବାରଣ means prevention or restraint (as in ରୋଗ ନିବାରଣ), and ନିର୍ବହଣ means carrying out or execution (related to ନିର୍ବାହ). In official Odia, ପ୍ରବର୍ତ୍ତନ is used for launching a scheme or a rule, for example 'ସରକାର ନୂଆ ଯୋଜନା ପ୍ରବର୍ତ୍ତନ କଲେ' (the government introduced a new scheme), and ପ୍ରବର୍ତ୍ତକ means founder or introducer. Memory tip: for antonyms of tatsama words, first look for an option with the same root and the same ending but the opposite prefix, as in ଉତ୍କର୍ଷ ↔ ଅପକର୍ଷ, ଉପକାର ↔ ଅପକାର and ଉନ୍ନତି ↔ ଅବନତି.

Q 115 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘ତେଲିଆ’ର ବିପରୀତ :
(1) ପାମ୍ପରା
(2) ଶୁଖିଲା
(3) ନୁଖୁରା
(4) ଅତେଲିଆ
= 3
~ ନୁଖୁରା ('without oil, dry and unoiled', said of the head, hair or skin) is the antonym of ତେଲିଆ ('oily, oiled'). Everyday usage shows the pair clearly: ତେଲିଆ ମୁଣ୍ଡ (an oiled head) ↔ ନୁଖୁରା ମୁଣ୍ଡ (an unoiled, dry head). ତେଲିଆ is formed from ତେଲ + the Odia suffix -ଇଆ, which makes adjectives meaning 'having or full of': ଲୁଣିଆ (salty), ବାଲିଆ (sandy), ପାହାଡ଼ିଆ (hilly). Such native Odia words usually have native Odia opposites rather than forms made with the Sanskrit negative prefix ଅ-, so ଅତେଲିଆ (option D) is an artificial form, not a standard word. ଶୁଖିଲା (option B) means dry in the sense of 'not wet'; its proper opposite is ଓଦା, so it is not the direct opposite of ତେଲିଆ. ପାମ୍ପରା (option A) is not used as the opposite of ତେଲିଆ either. Trap: ଶୁଖିଲା looks right because unoiled hair looks dry, but the exam wants the word that specifically means 'without oil'. Memory tip: picture a neatly oiled and combed head next to a ନୁଖୁରା head that has just come in from the sun.

Q 116 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘ଶିଷ୍ଟ’ର ବିପରୀତ :
(1) ଦୁଷ୍ଟ
(2) ଅଶିଷ୍ଟ
(3) ଖଳ
(4) ଅଭଦ୍ର
= 1
~ ଦୁଷ୍ଟ ('wicked, evil') is the antonym of ଶିଷ୍ଟ in the key. ଶିଷ୍ଟ (from the root ଶାସ୍, 'to instruct, to discipline') describes a person who is well-trained, cultured and virtuous, and in standard antonym lists it is paired with ଦୁଷ୍ଟ. The pair is familiar from the phrase 'ଶିଷ୍ଟ ପାଳନ, ଦୁଷ୍ଟ ଦମନ' (protecting the good and punishing the wicked), the idea of the Gita's 'paritrāṇāya sādhūnāṃ vināśāya ca duṣkṛtām' (4.8). ଅଶିଷ୍ଟ (option B) is only the negative form, 'uncultured, impolite', and it is close in meaning to ଅଭଦ୍ର (option D), the opposite of ଭଦ୍ର; since these two say almost the same thing, neither can be the single answer. ଖଳ (option C) means a villain or wicked person and is used mostly as a noun (as in ଖଳନାୟକ, the villain of a story). Related words: ଶିଷ୍ଟାଚାର (etiquette, good manners) and ଶିଷ୍ଟତା (courtesy). Do not confuse ଶିଷ୍ଟ with ଅବଶିଷ୍ଟ (remaining), which comes from a different root. Memory tip: ସଜ୍ଜନ ↔ ଦୁର୍ଜନ, ସୁଜନ ↔ କୁଜନ, ଶିଷ୍ଟ ↔ ଦୁଷ୍ଟ.

Q 117 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘ସ୍ୱାବଲମ୍ବୀ’ର ବିପରୀତ :
(1) ଅନାବଲମ୍ବୀ
(2) ପରାବଲମ୍ବୀ
(3) ନିରାବଲମ୍ବୀ
(4) ଅଣବଲମ୍ବୀ
= 2
~ ପରାବଲମ୍ବୀ ('dependent on others') is the antonym of ସ୍ୱାବଲମ୍ବୀ ('self-reliant'). Split them: ସ୍ୱ ('self') + ଅବଲମ୍ବୀ ('one who leans on, depends on') = ସ୍ୱାବଲମ୍ବୀ, and ପର ('another') + ଅବଲମ୍ବୀ = ପରାବଲମ୍ବୀ. In both, dīrgha sandhi joins ଅ + ଅ into ଆ, as in ହିମ + ଆଳୟ = ହିମାଳୟ. Only the first part changes (ସ୍ୱ ↔ ପର), which makes this the exact opposite; similar pairs are ସ୍ୱାଧୀନ ↔ ପରାଧୀନ and ସ୍ୱାର୍ଥ ↔ ପରାର୍ଥ. The nouns are ସ୍ୱାବଲମ୍ବନ (self-reliance) and ପରାବଲମ୍ବନ (dependence). The other options do not work: ଅନାବଲମ୍ବୀ and ଅଣବଲମ୍ବୀ are made-up negatives, and ନିରାବଲମ୍ବୀ, like ନିରାଲମ୍ବ, would mean 'without any support, helpless', a different idea from depending on others. Context: the women's self-help groups of Odisha's Mission Shakti and the national call for an ଆତ୍ମନିର୍ଭର ଭାରତ (self-reliant India) both have ସ୍ୱାବଲମ୍ବନ as their central idea. Memory tip: when the given word begins with ସ୍ୱ- ('self'), look for the option that begins with ପର- ('other').

Q 118 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘ଉତ୍କର୍ଷ’ର ବିପରୀତ :
(1) ଅନାକର୍ଷ
(2) ଅନୁତ୍କର୍ଷ
(3) ଅପକର୍ଷ
(4) ନିରୁତ୍କର୍ଷ
= 3
~ ଅପକର୍ଷ ('decline, deterioration, lowering') is the antonym of ଉତ୍କର୍ଷ ('excellence, rise, eminence'). Both come from the root କୃଷ୍ ('to draw, to pull') with opposite prefixes: ଉତ୍- ('up') gives ଉତ୍କର୍ଷ, 'drawing upward', and ଅପ- ('down, away') gives ଅପକର୍ଷ, 'drawing downward'. The adjectives follow the same pattern: ଉତ୍କୃଷ୍ଟ (excellent) ↔ ଅପକୃଷ୍ଟ or ନିକୃଷ୍ଟ (inferior). The same root also gives ଆକର୍ଷଣ (attraction) and ବିକର୍ଷଣ (repulsion). The other options are not standard words: ଅନାକର୍ଷ confuses the word with ଆକର୍ଷ, while ଅନୁତ୍କର୍ଷ and ନିରୁତ୍କର୍ଷ are artificial negatives of ଉତ୍କର୍ଷ that are not used as its antonym. Similar prefix pairs: ଉନ୍ନତି ↔ ଅବନତି, ଉପକାର ↔ ଅପକାର. Odisha link: the state's investors' meet held in Bhubaneswar in January 2025 was named 'Utkarsh Odisha – Make in Odisha Conclave', using ଉତ୍କର୍ଷ in its sense of rise and excellence. Memory tip: ଉତ୍ = up, ଅପ = down, so ଉତ୍କର୍ଷ ↔ ଅପକର୍ଷ.

Q 119 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘ଆରାଧ୍ୟ’ର ବିପରୀତ :
(1) ଅନାରାଧ୍ୟ
(2) ଦୁରାଧ୍ୟ
(3) ବିପ୍ରରାଧ୍ୟ
(4) ଦୁରାରାଧ୍ୟ
= 4
~ ଦୁରାରାଧ୍ୟ is the antonym of ଆରାଧ୍ୟ in the key. ଆରାଧ୍ୟ (ଆ + the root ରାଧ୍, 'to please, to propitiate') means 'worthy of worship', one who can be won over by worship. Adding the prefix ଦୁର୍ ('with difficulty') gives ଦୁର୍ + ଆରାଧ୍ୟ = ଦୁରାରାଧ୍ୟ, 'hard to please or propitiate'; the ର of ଦୁର୍ simply joins the following ଆ. Antonyms made with ଦୁର୍ are common in standard lists: ସୁଲଭ ↔ ଦୁର୍ଲଭ, ସୁଗମ ↔ ଦୁର୍ଗମ, ସୁଜନ ↔ ଦୁର୍ଜନ, ସୁକର ↔ ଦୁଷ୍କର. The other options are not accepted here: ଅନାରାଧ୍ୟ is only a plain negative ('not to be worshipped'), which the key does not take; ଦୁରାଧ୍ୟ drops the syllable ରା and has no proper meaning; and ବିପ୍ରରାଧ୍ୟ is not a word at all (ବିପ୍ର means a Brahmin). Related words from the same root: ଆରାଧନା (worship), ଆରାଧକ (worshipper) and ଆରାଧ୍ୟ ଦେବତା (the deity one worships), as Lord Jagannath is for most Odias. Memory tip: when a word has a pleasant or easy sense, adding ଦୁର୍- usually gives its opposite.

Q 120 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘ଖର୍ଚ୍ଚ’ ର ବିପରୀତ :
(1) ଜମା
(2) ସଂଚୟ
(3) ପୁଂଜି
(4) ସଂଗ୍ରହ
= 1
~ ଜମା ('deposit, amount received or saved') is the antonym of ଖର୍ଚ୍ଚ ('spending, expenditure'). The two are used together in everyday accounts as ଜମାଖର୍ଚ୍ଚ (receipts and expenditure), and an office cash book has a ଜମା side and a ଖର୍ଚ୍ଚ side. Both words came into Odia through Persian, so the pair matches in origin too; the matching Sanskrit pair is ଆୟ (income) ↔ ବ୍ୟୟ (expenditure). The other options are not direct opposites. ସଂଚୟ (standard spelling ସଞ୍ଚୟ) means savings or wealth built up over time; ପୁଂଜି (ପୁଞ୍ଜି) means capital invested in a business; ସଂଗ୍ରହ means collecting or gathering. Useful for a government-office exam: ଜମାବନ୍ଦି is the land-revenue record, and ଆୟ-ବ୍ୟୟ ହିସାବ is the statement of income and expenditure, as in a budget. Usage: 'ମାସ ଶେଷରେ ଜମା ଓ ଖର୍ଚ୍ଚ ମିଳାଇ ହିସାବ କର' (at the month's end, tally the deposits and the expenses). Memory tip: look for the word that appears in a fixed pair with the given word, such as ଜମା-ଖର୍ଚ୍ଚ, ଆୟ-ବ୍ୟୟ and ଲାଭ-କ୍ଷତି.

Q 121 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘କୁଆପଥର’ର ପ୍ରତିଶବ୍ଦ :
(1) କରକଚା
(2) କରକା
(3) କରଞ୍ଜ
(4) କରଉ
= 2
~ କରକା is the tatsama (Sanskrit) synonym of କୁଆପଥର, the hailstone: a lump of ice that falls from a storm cloud. Sanskrit lexicons also call it ବର୍ଷୋପଳ, 'rain-stone' (ବର୍ଷା + ଉପଳ, stone), which matches the Odia idea of a 'stone' falling from the sky. A hailstorm is called ଶିଳାବୃଷ୍ଟି; in Odisha it comes mostly between March and May with the Kalbaisakhi (nor'wester) storms and can damage crops and roofs. The other options do not mean hail. କରଞ୍ଜ is the karanja or Pongamia tree, whose seed oil is used in lamps, in medicine and for biodiesel. କରକଚା and କରଉ have nothing to do with hail. Trap: କରକଚା begins with the same letters as କରକା, so read the whole word before choosing. Note too that the masculine କରକ can mean a water-pot, such as an ascetic's kamandalu, so learn the feminine form କରକା specifically for hail. Link for the next question: ଉପଳ means stone, so ବର୍ଷୋପଳ and କୁଆପଥର describe the same thing in two languages.

Q 122 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘ସାପ’ ର ପ୍ରତିଶବ୍ଦ :
(1) ଉପଳ
(2) ଉଦକ
(3) ଉରଗ
(4) ଉରଜ
= 3
~ ଉରଗ is a Sanskrit synonym of ସାପ (snake). It is formed from ଉରସ୍ or ଉର ('chest') + ଗ ('one that goes'): the creature that moves on its chest. Many tatsama nouns end in -ଗ or -ଙ୍ଗ, 'mover', and the first part tells where or how the thing moves: ଖଗ and ବିହଙ୍ଗ (moves in the sky: bird), ତୁରଗ and ତୁରଙ୍ଗ (moves fast: horse), ଭୁଜଙ୍ଗ and ପନ୍ନଗ (snake), and ନଗ (that which does not move: mountain or tree). Other synonyms of ସାପ are ସର୍ପ, ନାଗ, ଫଣୀ, ଅହି and ବିଷଧର. The options form a sound-alike trap, since all begin with ଉ. ଉପଳ means stone (as in ବର୍ଷୋପଳ, hailstone); ଉଦକ means water (compare ଉଦଧି, ocean); ଉରଜ (ଉର + ଜ, 'born on the chest') denotes the breast, not a snake. Note the contrast in the last letter: -ଗ means 'goes' and -ଜ means 'born'. Memory tip: ଉରଗ = ଉର + ଗ, the 'chest-walker', just as ଖଗ is the 'sky-walker'.

Q 123 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘ଛାତ୍ର’ର ପ୍ରତିଶବ୍ଦ :
(1) ଅଧୀତୀ
(2) ଅନକ୍ଷ
(3) ଅଧିଭୂ
(4) ଅଧୃତି (ଅଧୃତି)
= 1
~ ଅଧୀତୀ is taken as the synonym of ଛାତ୍ର (student) in the key. It comes from ଅଧୀତ ('studied, learnt'), the past participle of ଅଧି + the root ଇ ('to go over, to study'), with the suffix -ଇନ୍, so it describes one who studies or has studied. The same root gives the familiar words ଅଧ୍ୟୟନ (study), ଅଧ୍ୟାପକ (teacher), ଅଧ୍ୟାୟ (chapter) and ଅଧ୍ୟେତା (one who studies). Other synonyms of ଛାତ୍ର: ଶିଷ୍ୟ, ବିଦ୍ୟାର୍ଥୀ, ଅନ୍ତେବାସୀ (one who lives with the teacher) and ପାଠୁଆ. The distractors look similar because they all begin with ଅ: ଅଧିଭୂ means a lord or ruler (ଅଧି, 'over' + ଭୂ, 'to be'); ଅଧୃତି means lack of steadiness or patience (ଅ + ଧୃତି); ଅନକ୍ଷ is unrelated to learning. Interesting origin: ଛାତ୍ର is traditionally linked with ଛତ୍ର (umbrella), because teacher and pupil shelter each other like an umbrella. Memory tip: ଅଧୀତୀ, ଅଧ୍ୟୟନ and ଅଧ୍ୟାପକ all share 'adhi + i', the root of study; but check the root, not just the look, because ଅଧ୍ୟକ୍ଷ (chairman) and ଅଧୀଶ (lord) come from other roots.

Q 124 | odi | Vocabulary & Usage
‘ଗଂଗା’ର ପ୍ରତିଶବ୍ଦ :
(1) ସ୍ୱର୍ଣ୍ଣଳୀ
(2) ସ୍ୱର୍ଣ୍ଣଦୀ
(3) ସରିତା
(4) ନନ୍ଦିକା
= 2
~ ସ୍ୱର୍ଣ୍ଣଦୀ is a name of the Ganga. It is ସ୍ୱର୍ ('heaven') + ନଦୀ ('river'), 'the river of heaven', recalling the story of the Ganga descending from heaven through Shiva's matted hair at Bhagiratha's request. After ର the ନ becomes ଣ (ṇatva rule), and Odia, as usual (ବର୍ଣ୍ଣ, କର୍ଣ୍ଣ), writes it as ର୍ଣ୍ଣ, so the word looks like ସ୍ୱର୍ଣ୍ଣ (gold). Trap: it does not mean 'golden river'; split it as ସ୍ୱର୍ + ନଦୀ, not ସ୍ୱର୍ଣ୍ଣ + ଦୀ. Other names of the Ganga: ଜାହ୍ନବୀ (daughter of the sage Jahnu), ଭାଗୀରଥୀ (brought down by Bhagiratha), ମନ୍ଦାକିନୀ, ସୁରଧୁନୀ, ଦେବନଦୀ, ତ୍ରିପଥଗା (flowing through the three worlds) and ବିଷ୍ଣୁପଦୀ. The other options fail: ସରିତା means any river, not the Ganga in particular, while ସ୍ୱର୍ଣ୍ଣଳୀ and ନନ୍ଦିକା are not names of the Ganga.
